LADIES' LETTER

MY DEAR NELLIE,

For the coming season tailor-made costumes are likely to be very fashionable, and it will gratify Tou to be made aware that plain materials are to be in vogue. These will include the favourite satin-face amazone cloths, French serge coatings and fine quality woollen poplins. It is well to observe that in most of the new varieties of coats the way in which the lapels and collars are shaped is especially noticeable. In many instances these are put on separately and guipure lace is e ..Len.- sively utilised for covering the collars, some examples of the coats having real lace collars, these appearing on foundations of richly tinted You will agree with me that such a fashion is one that makes for effectiveness in costume. By the way, coa.s are tight fitting and if one desires to be in the mode she must see to it that her skirts are made to fit tiehtly round the hips.

Headgear is an ever-present subject in min& feminine, and we all acknowledge that a neat chapeau is to be desired by every woman who take pride in her appearance. An exceedingly prett hat which I saw lately was trimmed with lar2E bows of velvet and graceful plumes. I learn that the very latest travelling hat is one of ths familiar toreador shape, trimmed with gauze ox ribbon.

Blouses are a 9 pienLirul as ever for autumn wear, and many beautitul examples have been noticed of late. Favourite fabrics seem to be a silky cash- mere or nun's veiling cf fine quality. These have the advantage of being comparatively inexpensive as well as highly attnlctive ; thay are obtainable in all the popular shades, and it i 4 possible for one to match or contrast atskirt. Toe sleeves, it should be said, are generally email ani tight, and a eilk collar and revere make a c.,oital finish.

The tunic overskirt is a delightful medium for the fashionable stitching, and it is a fashion which does not shorten the figure, unless violent contrasts of colour and material are employed. One which was brought under my notice quite recently was of golden-brown Amazon cloth, the bodice as a rounded bolero, outlined with lines of stitching, and reaching to the waist at the back. In front the bolero was supplemented by vest pieces of velvet, forming Eton points at tho waist, but not reaching to the neck. The collard-band and plastron of mastic surith were tucked all over. The skirt was made with a plain foundation, over which came a tight tunic running up in front, and outlined by stitching; attached to the edge of this was a secondary tunic drapery of velvet, which formed points at either side, and an elongated point at the back.

You will he interested in knowing that it is quite the exception now for veils to be worn by really smart people, awl this is a direct result of the present style of hat which, with its curious curves and (in many cases) strings, does not allow of satisfactory adjustment of the veil. It is also a difficult matter to arrange a veil realty becom. ingly over a toque, which, if made of soft tulle or chiffon, as are so many of these pretty head-cover. ings, is liable to be crushed and flattened even by the light confinement of a veil. When veils are worn now they are either of plain white tulle or very fine plain black veiling, or of a fine French mesh with very small chenille spots. Black veils lined with pink tulle, and styled complexion veils, have been introduced for some time now, but have not really caught on, as they have been found so dazzling to the eyesight. To return for a moment to ercsEcs, lot ma say that the new half-length coats look immensely comfortable for the late autumn and winter, and are very smart with a long, tight skirt. The great merit of this type of garment is that it can be worn with so many cliffz,:nt gowns, and without being close-fitting, it gives a straight and shapely outline. The front is not double-breasted, but fastens eligh tly to the left side with a single row of buttons. The small lapels turn out from the fronts, and meeL a broad turn.down collar, and these are faced with velvet.

Your loving

JIL

" WOMEN'S CHAT."

It is not much more than a year since the Hon. Su,an Baring, youngest daughter of Lord Revelstoke, was ap- pointed Maid of Honour to the Queen, and now comes the news that her marriage with Sir James Reid, Physician in Ordinary to the Queen, and the Prince of Wales, will shortly take place. Both her sisters married quite in the teens, the one Lord Castle- rosse, and the other Mr. " Bobby "Spencer. Miss Bar- ing is piquant and slight, with a very pretty manner, which is dignified without any stiffness.and she is gifted with enormous tact and sympathy. She is very much liked, and so is her sister Lady Castler _Bs.. Aprapos of weddings, Wednesday is more honoured by Hymen than any other day of the week. Tuesday is considered fairly tavourable as also is Monday. Thurs- day, Friday, and Saturday are the three least favoured days. Everybody has heard the ancient rhyme" Mon- day for wealth, Tuesday for health, Wednesday the best day of all ; Thursday for crosses, Friday for losses, Saturday no day at all." Even the colour of the wedding garment is shadowed by superstition. " They that marry in green their sorrow is soon seen." "Blue is true "the old rhyme goes, and that colour has been a favourite at weddings, since time began. Red is also a happy colour. Yellow is the synonym for jealousy,and was considered by the ancients an atrocious taste. Hymen, or Hymenaioe, in Grecian mythology, was the son of Apollo, and a Muse. In very early legeLd3 he was described as a mortal of extreme beauty, who delivered from captivity some Attic virgins who had been carried by Pelasgian pirates to Clenais. For this service he was rewarded with a maiden of whom he was deeply enamoured, and on account of his conduct and the happiness of his marriage, he weeinv ked in bridal songs, thence called hymenal songs. In ancient art, he is represented as a tall and elegant, but some- what effeminate youth. He usually carries in his hand the bridal torch, and sometimes wears on his head or around his neck a wreath of flowers. From time te time ludicrous incidents cr )p up at weddings' and one of the most amusing recsnt stories runs as follows. In a West of England church, where a good many weddings are solemnized, the service was being read by a curate a most estimable young clergy- man, who was greatly lik2d, but was apt occasionally to mistake. The best man, in this case, made all the re- sponses, and the clergyman was jug; about to unite him in the bonds of holy matrimony with the bride, when the verger happily intervened, and prevented a rather awkward complication. At the same church not so very long since, the vicar himself was solemnizing a marriage, the bridegroom being a soldier. After the ser- vice was concluded, the latter said to him, " You will have to trust me sir, for the fee." A provident marriage that!

_ The lot of woman can scarcely be more miserable I than that of her who marries a private soldier or junior non-commissioned officer" without leave." The men who marry these women know perfectly well that they are totally unable to support their wives, and that their lives must be one of considerable drudgery or worse. Of course it is egregious fully on the part of women to marry without leave, but there ought to be some means of punishing the men who deceive them. The extent to which the evil exists in every garrison town, constitutes a gross scandal, resulting in a serious charge upon the rates for the maintenance of women thus abandoned, yet no attempt is made to punish the ruffians who cause the trouble. Many of them marry again when they leave the army but we never hear of them being convicted for bigamy. Most of U 9 have heard that the American business man spends too much of his time at the office, and takes very little recreation. This application is generally supposed, at any rate in these Isles of ours, to be dice to a desire to mews dollars, but another explanation is afforded by a writer in one of the leading London papers, which devoted recently, more than a column to the subject of the American girl, of whom he seems to have a very high opinion. He says—The American " husband is so "sat on " at home, that he has got into the " way of spending very much more time at his business than "is the usual habit of the men in this country "! A writer in the " Queen " offers some sensible advice to wernen who may be going to Switzerland, and pro- pose spending a portion ef their time in mountaineering. " ely advise "she says " is to take as little as passible with ycu, and to pack that little in such small compess that you can carry it if necessary." The " douane "is one of the great drawbacks to leaving our country. If it is only hand luggage teat•has to be examined the annoyance is minimised,otherwise it entails halt-an-hour or an hour's waiting, and most disagreeable crowding and packing in uncomfortable waiting rooms. A process repeated three and some`imes four times en. route. What is really required as far as outfit is concerned is a well made, tailor cot gown in tweed, cloth, or serge, with a coat of course ; a short skirt for climbing with nicker- bockers to match, and a cloth cape, generally slung to the back by a strap, when walking over the mountains ready for use as required. In the matter of mountaineerinz, probably no livieg woman can compare with Miss Bristow, who was the first to climb the Matterhorn by the ordinary route, and descend by the Zmutt Ridge. She it was who taught the Alpine Club how difficult rocks should be scaled,and surrounded on three sides by nothing, she might frequently be seen sitting on ledges, taking photographs of other members of the party As they struggled up. For autumn and coming winter wear, plain fabrics are being eeteneively prepared, but there are some dellehtful items to relieve the monotony. The fancy cashnieres and woolen bengalie es show stripes in silks so potential that the material at the first glasice would seem to be made of superadded fabric: Others have lace stripes and minute spots. Vicuna remains with UP, and matelasse has come back afcer a somewhat new order with check effects. The market is flooded for the moment with materials of tartan mixture which find many American purchasers and there is a great rush for Seettish tweeds. From New York, there is consider- able demand for the thick make of crepons. Earrings have at length secured a firm footing in the world monistic and grow more and more in favour as time goes on. Moreover there is now no neesssity to bore holes in oneself, in which to hang this psrticuler form of ornament. The majority of the earrings are fitted with little blunt round headed screws so that the jewel can be attached to the ear in a more cemfortable manner.

" MADGE.'

FESTIVETIES AT THEL WALL

THE POLICE AND RAILWAY MEN AT CUE RDEN HALL.

Mr. R. A. Naylor and Miss Naylor seem destined I to become noted as the most liberal entertainers in the neighbourhood. It is only a few weeks since a party of over 500 connected with the St. John Ambulance Association was entertained at Cuetden Hall and on Thursday a body of Warrington polled officers accompanied by their wives and friends and also a portion of the staff at Bank Quay station received similar hospitality. Ihe weather though dull and threatening confirmed fine and the visitors spent a most enjoyable afternoon amid the rural beauties of Mr. Naylor's residence. After an inspec- tion of the hall with its wealth of beauty and elegance, the party was free to ramble through the grounds or induige in games on the lawn. Permis- sion was given to relieve the fruit trees of some of their burden and this privilege was much appreciated. The police and their friends numbered 40. The party in- cluded the Chief Cone table and his wile (Mr. and Mrs. Luke Talbot), Chief Inspector Phillips, Inspector Row- bottom, Sergearitr Cheuey, Holling worth and Dean. There were about the same number of railway employ e from Bank Quay. The company was joined by a large house party including Dr. and Mrs. Dyer of Cheltenham Rev. Canon and Mrs. Davies. of Pwllhelli; Miss Stannus, of L ndon ; Mrs Grimshaw, of Hale Barnes ; Mr and Miss Nixon, of Hale; Mr and Mrs R M Prescot.of Nelson and Miss Stanley. An excellent tea was provided in the Temperance Mission Hall. At the coLclusion of the repast The Rev. Carroll DAVIES said there was one duty to perform and that was to propose a hearty vote of thanks to Mr. and Miss Naylor f..r their hospitality. After re- ferring in complimentary terms to Mr. Naylor's generosity he said there was only one drawback ; he was an 011 bachelor—(laughter)—but he was a good old bachelor. (Laughter.) He took delight in doing good and also looked to the spiritual welfare of his neigh- bours. He (Canon Davies) was glad to find that he advocated temperance. The Canon proceeded to refer to the benefits of total abstinence, and advised his hearers to keep clear of drink. There was always danger in connection with drink. If they wanted to have a race of sober men they must attend to the chil- dren. There was nothing like inculcating temperance principles amongst children and he was glad Mr. Naylor took special pains and trouble to do so. The drink traffic, he contended, was the curse of the nation. The CHIEF,CONSTABLE said it was with great pleasure he rose to second the vote of thanks to Mr. and Miss Naylor, which had been so eloquently proposed by the Rev. Canon Davies. He was sure they had all enjoyed , themselves exceedingly. The beautiful fresh air was a great contrast to the atmosphere of Warrington at the present time, and he was sure their outing must have done them a great deal of good. The social intercourse of that gathering would no doubt be productive of good feeling amongst the men and their friends hereafter,if that good feeling did not exist already. Everything they bad had and seen bad been very enjoyable. The very excellent homily they had just listened to would not have done them any harm ,but be might state to the friends present, that a more steady and sober lot of men did not exist in any police force in England than was to be found in Warrington (Applause.) Their friends from the railway were also. known to be a steady and sober lot of men. (Hear, hear.) He would conclude by seconding the vote of thanks.

The proposition was unanimously carried. Miss NAYLOR, in acknowledging the vote, said she was very thankful for the hearty vote of thanks. She had been very glad indeed to see them: (Applause.) Mr. R. A. NAYLOR also responded and said he wished to say how very glad his sister and himself were to see so many present on that occasion. They hoped it would not be the last: (Hear, hear.) He did not know whether it was correct but he was told that Warrington was in a very unhealthy state at the present time and it was well that they should csme there and breathe a little fresh air. (Hear, hear.) Warrington aas in a very bad way in respect to other things besides sick- ness. He had never had the pleasure of becoming a member of the Town Council because he had declined to accept any such position. They ought not to say too much about those gentlemen who gave their time and attention to the affairs of the ancient Borough of Warrington, but at the same time it might be as well if they would be a little more friendly disposed towards one another, than they seemed to be. He understood that they had been using extrava- gant language (Laughter.) Such only led to one thing, it generally ended in blows, and he should not be at a!• surprised if he heard ef a little bit of " fisty-cuffs " takicg place in the Warrington Town Council Chamber. (Laughter.) It was a great misfortune when the gcntle- men who represented their interests had not the common sense to behave when they met together. like English gentlemen ought to do. (Hear, Lear.) He could only endorse the words which bad fallen from the lips of the Chief Constabla.that the officers of Warrington were example to many. The same remark applied.to theirfriends connected with the railway. He thought they would agree with him tt at their conduct was of an exemplary character and they were always most civil and obliging. (Applause.) An adjoarnment was afterwards made to the hall, where a brief chamber concert was provided. The aud- ience was accommodated in the gallery of the entrance hall. Dr. Dyer gave a brillant pianoforte recital which called forth the hearty appreciation of those present. Mr. and Miss Naylor also favoured the company with a duet and Mr. Naylor further delighted his hearers by rendering " A fine old Eagli,la Gentleman." Subs,- quently a return visit was paid to the Temperance Hall where a large company of villagers had assembled to witness the distribution of prizes in the cottage garden- ing and other competitions. Mr. Naylor presided and briefly referred to the results of the competition,and the poaslbilities to be attained. He then called upon Mrs. Davies to distribute the prizes. The prize winners in the cottage gardening competition were :-Ist, Mr J Simpson, £1 is; 2nd, Mrs Leigh, 103 ; 3rd, Mr Fredeaux, ss; 4th, Mrs Leah, 2s 6d. A number of money prim s were also awarded to children attending the Bacd of Hope for regular attendance and good conduct. The prize winners were : Mary Phcenix, Annie Smith, Albert Leigh, Joe Leigh, Frank Ditchfield, James Hankinson. and Robert Hankinson.

Mr. Sherwood of Warrington afterwards delighted the company with a conjuring entertainment. At the close coffee and buns were handed round, and an enjoyable evenine's entertainment concluded.

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY SEPTEMBER 15, 1899

(Copyright.)

THE

REVENGE OF VALERIE:

A ROMANCE OF BRITISH COLUMBIA.

BY HIJAIE NISBET,

Author of "A Sweet Sinner," "Bail Up," "The Rebel Chief," "Kings of the Sea," "Hunting or Gold, "The Swampers," tee.

expected tto Kiss a Wm:Unary Daby. 'Them kind ain't used to nussing and cuddling babies. They've got servants to do this duty for'em. Never mina, my precious, you've got a good friend now, and if she don't look kind she acts all right." Mrs. Hambly went no more to the wash-tub except to do up her own and Lewis John's linen. With pardonable pride she announced to her customers that she was now bought up by a Duchess. _ _ _

Being a Duchess, it appeared like blasphemy in Mrs. Hambly's opinion to judge her patroness ,from the standard of womanhood. Yet she wished that the lady had shown more interest in her ad)pted son, and just a little sympathy and pity for his real mother. She grew very melancholy when she thought about that poor mother's terrible fate. The prison was bad enough for one so sensitive and good to endure. But to have to give up and part for ever from her darling boy, Mrs. Hambly could not conceive any doom more severe and heartrending. "I've heard about them poor prisoners in Siberia or some such place, where it's always hice and snow. I've heard about the Hamerican slaves, but they're not in it with this trial. Why, I'd sooner go to the gallows right off and be 'anged than do it. How I ham to stand by and watch that parting I don't know, I'm sure. Poor gal, poor hinnocent gal, for I knows you never went and done it. May the Lord 'ave mercy on you, for He's put it heavy on you, and no mistake about it." Next day her Grace's man of business called upon the widow and startled her by the munificence of the terms he proposed. She had never dreamt, even in her most palmy days, of being worth such wages. No more need for cheeseparing and trying to make ends meet. They were overlapping by yards now. She felt•herself a lady of means now, all owing to that blessed baby, Lewis John. All these creature comforts made it harder for her to meditate upon the poor prisoner in goal. The afternoon following the satisfactory visit of the lawyer, a great pile of packages were delivered at the cottage addressed to her. When she opened these packages one after the other, her feminine heart danced merrily, until she again thought about that poor prisoner. The Duchess had purchased for the baby as only a Duchess with an unlimited purse could purchase. The most complete and costly outfit that the West End could furnish came out of those bundles. The finest of cambric and linen. The most delicate of laces and handsome long robes and short dresses, mantles, caps, and the host of baby requirements, which, being only a man, the writer of this history cannot describe or enumerate. It requires the pen of a young lady author to do justice to it. Her Grace had done her duty. She had pur- chased for her adopted son as costly and extrava- gant an outfit as if he had been her own flesh and blood. Perhaps it was only because she enjoyed the shopping and had no need to deny herself the pleasure of buying. It may have been as a kind of salve to her conscience for having robbed the child of his mother. Perhaps to reconcile this victim to her sacrifice when she next saw her baby so well taken care of. Perhaps for his adored father's sake, for although she hated both child and mother, still that baby was portion of the man whom she worshipped with all the mad passion of her Oriental heart. _ _

Who can tell what blended reasons the Duchess had for her extravagant outlay. Possibly she hoped that the sight of her baby clothed as she could not have clad him would serve as another stab in that already sorely lacerated heart. At any rate, the sight of her child—the last sight which Valerie meant her enemy ever to have—would accentuate the fact of how idle it was for her to struggle against her destiny. Her happiness and her life Valerie now held with an iron grasp.

Those that were invited congratulated Memselves on their good fortune. A twelvemonth's freedom The people who were_ not _inyited wished that they from duns and other harassing worries; the best of wines, cigars and cooking; almost absolute freedom of action, and a renewed lease of life. The people who were not invited wished that they had been more friendly with Captain Eric St. Arnoul, impecunious blackleg although he was. As they idly wished this, they cursed their own want of foresight. "Politeness where Eric St. Arnoul is concerned cannot be said to cost nothing, yet we might have managed him with a few pounds and bee' in this also." Most of the society spectators thought this as they gathered to that farewell champagne luncheon at Cowes, and after- wards waved their hats and handkerchiefs from the tender.

The Duchess Valerie, knowing the censorious world, never placed herself in any ambiguous position. When she was talked about in society it was in terms of praise and admiration. She studied all the social convenances very closely, and verily she had her reward. She was respected, not alone for her wealth and position, but also for her decorous behaviour. Whererever she was located she patronised the recognised church, and gave largely to charities—i.e., public charities. Thus she was considered to be a perfectly safe companion for the most straight-laced and con- servative. She was received without demur into the innermost circle, and considered crime de la crime amongst the different circles and skimmings that take place on the top of the milk pail. Her city speculating proclivities were not, :of course considered at all 'derogatory to her dignity. In fact, she was all the more petted and courted by her own kind for dabbling in these matters. Dowagers and noble patricians, also venerable dignitaries, courted her assiduously for tips about the market; in fact, her outer apartments, while she had been at the Hotel Cecil, hadibeen as much thronged by society grandees as if they had been the ante- rooms of some great Court. Since the Colonial Premiers had occupied this luxurious and palatial Hotel, never had been seen such an eager crowd, or so many emblazoned carriages in its courtyard. For this present outing she had managed to secure the presence of one of the most highly-respected Bishops, and a brace of Court dowagers whose reputation alone would have sanctified any collection of Yahoos, The Bishop, like the pair of dowagers, had been ordered to lay in large supplies of ozone to recruit them after the influenza. They were not at all sorry to be able to obey their physician at the expense of her Grace. _ _ _ _

The other guests, before being invited, had been privately left to the selection of thee watch-dogs of society. A large list had been given to their inspection, with the numbers stated that the young Duchess required for the party. In this way Valerie won their sanction and approval by placing herself and her guests under their protection. At the same time, as she had made out the list her- self she succeeded in getting together the most select and agreeable people she knew. There was nothing omitted to make them all comfortable and happy. There wasn't a detrimental amongst them. The single people had been chosen with a discriminating eye towards future possi- bilities. Anxious mothers at home sighed with satisfaction now, to think that their dear girls were removed from temptation for so long a time. They could take their ease as they thought that their darlings were in such eligible company and under such experienced care. The married people, also, were not likely to cause any disagreeable scene as married pairs are sometimes likely to do when forced to endure each other's society. The Duchess and her able advisers had prevented all this by only inviting those wives whose husbands were in other parts of the world hunting, soldier- ing, or engaged in diplomatic pursuits, and those husbands who could not possibly bring their better halves. By this system complete harmony was assured, and a very pleasant prospect was before them all. This is how society manages to prevent those vulgar and sordid domestic scenes which so often spoil proletarian pleasure parties. Unlike Noah's ark, the Gadfly did not carry pairs. It carried odd sets of those that Hymen had already paired. They were all polite and attentive to each other.. The wedded people, possibly the most supremely satisfied that they had left their responsibilities and troubles behind them; the single as yet undecided whether they were likely to be happy or wretched about the arrangements made on their behalf. But a long voyage was before them, and propinquity was likely to smooth over all the present rough edges. If some thought that they had left their aristocratic hearts behind them in ineligible hands, if at present they were disposed to sulk against the obvious intentions of those stately high-priestesses of Hymen's temple, they would not be likely to kick very long against their destinies. _

Thus they started their journey under the care of the most dignified of spiritual and social pro- tectors, with the most talented of chefs and doctors to attend to their material wants, and a ship the acme of splendour, comfort, and safety. It was a cunningly-contrived ship in respect to economy of space. Although the cabins were neces- sarily small each was so arranged as to be trans- formed into sitting, bed, or dressing room at will of the inmates. By touching certain knobs, beds disappeared and satin-covered couches occupied their place, with all the dainty brw-à-brac of a lady's boudoir. On the gentlemen's side of the ship the same contrivances changed their berths into cosy bachelor dens, where they were able to enjoy as much privacy as they cared for. Knobs of electric buttons were placed in each cabin, with small labels above each button stating what it was to be pressed for. A touch and the article required instantly appeared on its own neatly-arranged buffet. By these arrangements the duties of the stewards and stewardesses were considerably lightened. These attendants also were marvels in their lines of duty, for each was either a well- trained and long-tried ladies'-maid or valet, and all models of discretion. Electric globes brilliantly lighted up the ship in the form of flowers and other artistic designs, producing an effect entrancing in its loveliness. The saloon was a dream of splendour, and like the private cabins, could be changed into either a dining or a drawing-room almost with the quick- ness of a transformation scene. Then the morocco- covered chairs were replaced by exquisite satin and silk-covered lounges, ottomans and chairs, while the larger dining tables disappeared and card and other tables took their places. Marble baths were supplied with hot and cold water and aromatic perfumes. The smoke-room cc:Add be used as a billiard-room as well, while the promenade-decks had silken awnings over them and palms set in pots that made them like a conservatory. Forty guests could be accommodated without crushing, but the Duchess had limited her guests to twenty, thus giving each of them a spare cabin to use as a store-room or wardrobe. The quarters, also, for the officers and sailors and firemen were as comfortable and commodious as they could be. The captain was an ex-naval commander. His officers also had been in the naval service, so that they knew, not only how to manage a ship, but how to look after the guns. There were three of these, one of long range and two gatlings. The Gadfly was thus prepared to sting as well as to skim along the ocean, if stinging was required. (To be continued.)

CHAPTER XIX.

LEWIS JOHN IS PROVIDED FOR.

Now that the ruin of her rival was accomplishea there was nothing more for the Duchess Valerie to do in England. However, as we have seen, she was a woman who kept her vows and promises, whether these were baleful or beneficent. Having, there- fore promised to adopt the child, she lost no time in doing this. Her first step in this direction was to visit Mrs. Hambly and see Lewis John. ::Valerie did not care much for babies, but even if she had loved them this particular infant would have been an excep- tion. As she gazed with sombre eyes upon this innocent and smiling token of her rival's powers, the spirit of murder crept into her jealous heart. To grip that soft and milky throat, or press her hand over those smiling moist rose lips, would have been a passionate and delirious joy. She shuddered with the strength of her desire to quench that budding life, and turned her gaze away. "I presume that you are fond of the child," she said, with a forced smile parting her dry lips. "Yes, ma'am." Mrs. Hambly was not aware of the rank of her visitor. Valerie had merely intro- duced herself as an old friend of the father and mother. "Yes, ma'am. I am fond of all children, but Lewis John here is like one of my own to me. His poor, unfortunate mother knew she could trust him to me."

"In that case you had better continue to look after him, if you think his mother would like you better than a stranger to be his nurse." "I am sure she would, ma'am, poor dear victim that she is."

"I have promised his mother to adopt him, and bring him up as a gentleman, therefore I wish you to devote all your time to his management. Can you do this if I make it worth your ;kilo ? " "Yes, ma'am. Laundry work ain't so easy or profitable for anyone to let it stand in the way of a better job." "Then that is decided," said the Duchess indifferently. "You are in my service after this, and your sole duty will be to attend to this infant. You must spare no expense, but get everything that he may require. My solicitor will pay you what salary you fix your services at, also settle all bills and accounts. HaNe you a good doctor here ? " _

"Yes, ma'am. Dr. Aimhttrst is the best man and the cleverest doctor in England, barring none." Valerie smiled more broadly than before. "You can employ Dr. Aimhurst while you and the child stay in Londo;, but I wish you to take him to the country as soon as possible, where he may have fresh air. Not just yet, but as soon as the weather will permit. My solicitor will take a house for you when you have decided where you would like to live. This good doctor will advise you. I think that is all we need arrange at present." She thought for a moment as she sat drumming her gloved fingers on the table, and then she said suddenly : "By the way, it will be best for this child, and his mother also, not to see each other more than is strictly needful. I wish him to grow up under the impression that his mother is dead." "0 ma'am, surely you ain't going to part mother and child. She is innocent as this baby of what she was convicted of. I can swear that, knowing her as I do, and as you must also, if you are art old friend of hers." _ _ _

..,. The Duchess looked, with a slight puckering of her arched brows, at Mrs. Hambly. While she frowned she registered another vow in her mind, which was that she would get rid of this officious old woman, as soon as she could find a more suitable nurse for the brat. She said, however, smoothly: "There are many good reasons why this child Xould not know anything about his mother when he grows up. Reasons which his mother fully understands and endorses."

"It will brcak 'er 'elrt, poor gal," answerod the kindly old woman, with a catch in her voice, while her eyes grew misty. "Never have I seen any mother so bouncpkv in 'er blessed baby as this one's mother was. Lthe'd go through fire and water to save him from a scratch. Yes, ma'am, it'll break 'er 'eart, as it a'most do mine to think of it. Pardon me, ma'am, for seeming to hobject, and kindly excuse them tears. But if you could have seen that y-ow.ig lonely wife and mother with her baby. How she watched him afilling hout as if it 'ad been the most wonderful work in creation—which it is all the same—t he growing of a baby from a little bit of red shapele,sness into a blooming cherub. Had you seen how she watched these little legs and harms Milling out until he became what he is now, a butter-ball of creamy whiteness, softness and beauty. Look at them harms, ma'am, and them fat kicking legs, did you hever see the likes of them for shapeliness and cunning dimples? Look at them wrists and hankies ; them little loves of 'ands and them purty feet. Could any woman let this go from her arter she had once howned it, kissed it and cuddled it to her bosom ? " The old lady was not at the present moment able to see the charms she was so freely exhibiting to those unsympathetic and loathing eyes. She could see neither the baby that she was wetting with her hot, tears, nor the woman she was so vainly appeal- ing to in behalf of the absent mother. And the baby lay placidly in her lap, sucking at its bottle of milk while its sky-blue large eyes stared vaguely out on space. The Duchess sat impatiently drumming her gloved fingers on the table, until Mrs. Hambly paused for want of breath rather than lack of subject. Then she said: "I have arranged that the mother should see the child. once more, and once more only. An intima- tion, with an order to visit her in prison, will be sent to you, (n the next visiting day, a month hence. I shall order some nice baby things and have them sent on to you. Then you can take him down so that she may bid him farewell." Something in the tones of the Duchess dried the tears that were flowing so freely, and arrested the harangue that was about to burst from Mrs. Hambly. "Whoever this lady is," thought the good woman, "she's a 'ardened one, as nothing I can say will melt." "Very well, ma'am, I will obey your horders," she said meekly, wiping her eyes with a corner of her apron. "You may write, if you like, to the mother, and assure her that the child is well and thriving." "Please, ma'am, that's more nor I can do. School Boards wasn't invented in my young days." "My solicitor will write for you in that case. He will call upon you and arrange with you to- morrow most likely."

She rose as she spoke and took out her pocket- case.

"Won't you kiss the purty dear, ma'am ? " "No! lam not used to babies, but there is a little present for him, and also something for you." She placed two crisp twenty-pound bank notes on the table.

"May I ask your name, ma'am ? " "There is my card and London address."

"I am sorry, ma'am, but I cannot read any more than I can write. My heducation never got so far."

"Well, I am the Duchess Valerie di Idumea, and my present address for the rest of this week will be the Hotel Cecil. After that I intend going for a long voyage. My solicitor will, however, arrange everything with you." Mrs. Hambly almost dropped her precious bundle at the unexpected honour vouchsafed to her. She started to her feet horrified that she had dared to sit in such a presence. _

"'Eaven help me, aa'am—your Duchess-ship, I mean. I 'opes as 'ow you'll kindly hoverlook my seeming want of respeck ? " "Good-bye, Mrs. Ilambly," said Valerie, graciously bending her head as she passed out smiling, leaving the worthy washerwoman open- mouthed and speechieSs staring after her. "Well I never. Who'd 'ave thought it!" said the honest woman to her unconscious charge, as soon as the beautiful and splendid vision had passed out of her sight. "A real, live Duchess taking up Lewis John. Why your fortune's made, my putty lambkin. I might ha' known though, if I had only thought about it, that she was sommut out of the hordinary. She was so beautiful and so hicy cold and dignified. Of course. a Duchess couldn't he

CHAPTER XX.

TIIE GADFLY.

The steam yacht Gadfly was the most perfect and beautiful production that money could purchase or shipbuilding craft turn out. To term it a yacht, in the strict sense, was decidedly a misnomer. A fairy-like cross between a pleasure steamer, an ocean liner, and a naval gunboat more strictly described it. It had all the aft state and luxury of the most up-to-date of ocean liners. All the comforts of those huge floating hotels on a cottage or bungalow scale. A compact and most refined and tasteful floating cottage as fashionable ladies comprehend cottage life. Of remarkably light draught considering its size, it was a model of strength, completeness, speed and beauty. The captain and crew were specially picked men of tried abilities and experience. Each sailor on board held a master's certificate, and nearly every man also was qualified to act as pilot in different parts of the navigable world. The Duchess Valerie gave captain's wages to her ordinary sea- men, and insisted on having none but experienced skippers to serve her. It had not been difficult either for her to secure this crew of young, select and certificated men. In every vessel that sails out of the ports there are in the forecastle captains and mates forced to accept the duties and wages of A. B. 's. These men have all the experience, all the training, all the know- ledge, and all the talents required to manage a ship and control a crew. One thing only they lack, and that is influence. Therefore, they are forced to knuckle down and often take their orders from a callow but hopeful young fellow who only knows as yet enough to have scrambled through his examina- tion. The experienced sailor and the experienced soldier without influence learn to accept the positions which destiny assigns to them, as do many other men in other professions learn to obey their young and unsophisticated masters. Socrates dearly loved his jest, yet, being a poor man all his life, he learnt to wrap it—the joke— within many folds of flossy silk. He loved his young masters of Athens also, who praised the floss wrappings round his hidden jest; who answered his modest questions without suspicion. Ah! Socrates was a true philosopher. He apologised to everyone he had offended, until, sick of Xantippe, sick of Athens, and life in general, he refused to apologise to the gods. Therefore, he took the hemlock and delivered himself of a few free senti- ments before he passed on to the Unknown. As the sage Socrates did, so do men, when they can so longer endure their insolent masters. The Duchess Valerie, in choosing the crew of her steam ship Gadfly, did not study influence. With her astute business acumen, she made three con- ditions only. That their record should be a good one; that their certificates were bond-fide, and that they could produce evidence of their experience and proficiency in home and foreign waters. She required practical, not gaseous seamen. She wanted also men whom she could herself trust and place confidence in. Her heart and fancy were centred on one man only, therefore she did not pick the most attractive applicants who had passed before her. In this she did not act according to her sex's usual custom; but then she owned a valuable vessel, and was besides a half-daughter of Israel. The Duchess Valerie conducted her own business on such occasions, and carefully examined each applicant. If the credentials satisfied her, she deputed her private detective to discover all about his proclivities and antecedents. To the officers she had chosen she left the duty of finding out whether this applicant could substantiate his credentials. Acting on these principles, she had secured perhaps the bravest and finest body of men, from the ordinary seaman up to the skipper, from the stoker up to the chief engineer, that it was possible for any ship to carry. The Gadfly had cost her a fortune to fit up, but at Cowes and elsewhere it was a thing of pride and joy to the ocean initiated. The crew also were sans reproche, and the fair owner did not cavil about their price. The skipper and officers would not have exchanged their berths for any other craft that floated. In the Gadfly they had something to officer which drew their hearts out. The crew might have desired to command the Gadfly, but they would rather serve in her than command a less perfect craft. The Duchess Valerie, as she selected her sub- ordinates, also chose, with the same wise foresight, her guests. Her cousin Eric St. Arnoul she had pardoned for his former shortcomings, and as he was her closest kin, she invited him first to join her in her expedition. To please him and make the time pleasantly she selected acquaintances who were either distinguished for their social qualities or for their exploits in the fields of war, adventure and sport. They were going to regions where steady nerves and cheerful dispositions were likely to be of more service than pedigree. It is needless to describe in detail the passengers of the Gadfly when she left England. Suffice it to say that both lady and gentlemen adventurers were eminently well-bred and agreeable people, also that they all felt delighted at the privilege which had been proferred to them. They were going on a most delightful voyage, with no settled programme. Their hostess was the raost charming and lavish of entertainers. All their desires and ideas of comfort would be gratified on board that magical craft, and at the end of this delicious trip each had the prospect of making their fortune on the cold-fields.

In connection with the above competition we may state that we offer a monthly prize of 5s to the competitor who succeeds in placing himself at the head of the Solvers' List at the end of the month. Where the prize is awarded the winner's score is cancelled, while the remaining competitors' points go towards the next month's competition. The win- ner may re-enter the competition if he chooses, commencing again at the bottom of the list. Points are awarded as follows : Two for the correct solu- tion of a problem, two for showing a dual solution, two for correcting the author's terms, and one for each minor correction. Intending cmpetitors may commence at any time.

Jordan says of the proposed Barker match—" It will be much better for me to come home first, as to go via San Francisco, accompanied by Mrs. J., means about £53 for rail fares to Chicago, and as they can only offer one engagement, they prefer that course themselves. I should like to visit America, but I don't see why it should be taken to benefit Barker, who declines to allow the usual expenses. He seems te imply that the various centres won't guarantee anything unless I consent to play him on his own terms: If this is so, why, I would rather play the match in Edinburgh, where I would be quite agreeable to allow him expenses, and I can visit America, after- wards if successful. lam writing to Mr. M.'Ateer to whom I am likely to suggest the Ferrie-Jordan or Stewart-Jordan style of restriction. This about. covers all he says upon this subject. Going on to speak of the colonial players, he says— "l have met pretty nearly all the cracks except Mar. There is some talk of a subscription match but Mar is only inclined to play friendly games and rm not. Boreham, of New Zealand, was going to play me for the championship, but after he lost abeut 10 to Ihe thought better of it. I played 31 games with Brodie-12 to 0, 19 drawn Last night I played 21 games simultaneous—won 20, 1 draw. Passey and Abrahams were both playing, and lost. Mrs. Jordan has gone on to Melbourne, where I will join her in two weeks, as I must be there in time for town and country match. I did not care much for New Zealand,but it is a splendid place for business. The celonies on the whole are a splendid place for working men, especially unskilled labourers. In New South Wales labourers on Government relief works have a standard wage of 6s per day, and anyone settling do v% n can live as cheaply as nt ',erne. Remind me to all friends."— Liverpool Mercury.

THE SURVEYOR'S EXPENSES.

The ASSISTANT CLERK reported that he bad examined the Surveyors c t9h book for the three fortnights endinz August 5, 19, and September 2, and he found it to be eorroet. The total amount expeacisd on wages Was £232 1818 d and on team labour £146 63 51, total £379 53 ld.—Tae report was ariopted.

Committee it was decided that the Surveyor should be instructed to communicate t 3 the workmen of the Coun- cil, that on and after Monday, August 21st, the hours of work would be on ordinary week-days, 6.30 a.m., to 5.30 p.m., and on Saturday, 6.30 a.m., to 4 p.m., half-an-hour boing allowed for breakfast, and one hour for dinner in each day. The SURVEYOR said he had received a petition from the workmen of the Council which said that they did no agree with the new arrangement, They desired walking time for work which was at a distance of over two miles, and they also objected to work until 4 o'clock on Saturday afternoon. They found that all the works in the district ceased work at one o'clock, and that they had holidays during the year, whereas the council's workmen did not enjoy the same privileges. They could not be classed with agricultural labourers. They res- pectfully asked the Council to reconsider their thcision. Mr. TRamesox pointed out that the petition was signed by thirty-five workmen, and they included all with the exception of one, Mr. KNOWLES made a suggestion that the men be al- lowed to start work at 6 o'clock and that they be al- lowed a quarter of an hour per mile walking time for over two miles and that they finish at one o'clock on Saturday. On the short days of the year he suggested that they should be employed until four o'clock on Saturday. Mr. J. HALTON said he had had a conversation with the surveyor respecting the time sheets of the men and the surveyor promised that he would advise a scheme which would meet the difficulties within a short period. He suggested that the matter should be left over for a month and then Mr. Thompson could also asuggeet a scheme for working hours as well as for the time sheets and the arrangement for payment of wages. Mr. THORNTON suggested that they should do away with the present system altogether of employing the roadmen, and let the work out on contract. He be- lieved it would prove e nnon3ical to th.s Council in the end.

The SURVEYOR said that the contract system was alto- gether impossible. Mr. HALTON moved a reslution that the matter be adjourned for a mlntb. and that in the meantime the Surveyor be instructed to formulate a scheme dealing with the difficulties in question. Mr. BROOKES seconded. . _

Mr. TFURSTON gave notice that he should move a re- solution that the work be done by contract. Mr. Elalron's resolution was carried, and the Council rose.

Mr. KER-I'OlT in his report stated that witn reference to the nuisance reported at the Catholic school,Martins- croft, he had had an interview with the Rev. Father Lowe, and he had decided to have the privies converted to the pail system. Since the last met ting the Medical Officer of Health ha l reported two deaths from infec- tious disease, one from diptheria at the Fiddle-i'th'-Bag, Burtonwood„and the other from typhoid at Woolston. He had examined the premises but failed to find any sani- tary defects to account for the disease. Both houses had since been disinfected. Mr. Robert Garnett had written to him complaining of the stench from the sewer at Penketh, also adrain,the property of the Council ,empty- hag foul matter into the brook,and a quantity of garbage manure lying on the canal bank at Fiddler's Ferry. He bad to report two houses in the township of Penketh as be!ng without a proper supply of wholesome water for the use of the occupiers, and 'would suggest that notice be given to the owners to connect with the Cor- poration water main. In consequence of complaints received of the bad quality of butter sold in a pro- vision shop in the Council's district, he purchased a sample under the Sale of Food and Drugs Act and for- warded it to J. Carter Bell for analysis and he certified it to be pure butter. 4 certificate under the Pablic Health Water Act 1878, had been granted to Dr. Bennett for a new house erected at Paegate. THE WARRINGTON CORPORATION TO BE SERVED WITH A NOTICE.

Mr. KNOWLES referred to a nuisance at Lengford caused by the Warrington Corporation. He understood from the Surveyor that the nuisance was caused by a defective drain which the Corporation emptied into the brook. It was a matter which could be easily attended to, and would pievent an offensive smell. He suggested that the Surveyor be instructed to serve a notice on the Corporation, for the abatement of the nuisance. The CLERK : We can compel them to attend to that matter. _ _

_ Mr. KNOWLES : They ar3 responsible for a penalty of £5 if they do not attend to the matter. It was decided that the notice should be served on the Corporation.

The CLERK reported with regard to the letter, dated August 24th. 1899, received from Mr. Shirley, Clerk to the Urban District Council of Newton in-Makerfield, re the cc-operation and assistance of the Warrington Coun- cil in the opening up of a carriage road from Burton- wood to Earlestown. On the 15th October, 1897, he reported to the late Highway Board that it would be better to leave the matter to this Council, seeing that the Board's existence was drawing to a close. The report was acted upon, hence the presence application. The road the Newton Council wished the Council to adopt was almost the whole of the private occupation road from Newton Common Lock to Hall-lane, Burton- wood, having a length of about 1599 yards and a width of from lb to 25 feet. A large sum of money would be required to place the road in the condition of a public highway and, considering the very tew that would be benefitted thereby, he could not advise the Council to accede to the desire of the Newton Council. Mr. KNOWLES asked if it was meant to continue the road from Mr. Longton's Farm through Yule .n into Earleitown ? The CLERK: That is not the road in question. Mr. KN3WLES (continuing) said the presence of the m.mbers was ; but he was told that on that road there was more traffic than on the highway to Dowton. He was there an Saturday and he was astonished at the enormous traffic. Mr. FORSHAW : There is a 'great deal of traffic on that road. Mr. KNOWLES (continuing) said it was a very danger- ous road under the London and North Western Railway Bridge and also at the Bridge over the brook. If the Newton authArity would co-operate with them in levell- ing and making that a better road and the Vulcan Foundry Company would also 3 assist them he thought they wouLt b 3 doing a work that had been waned for a long time. • Mr. WILLIAM HOUGHTON said he believed part of the road belonged to the Vulcan Foundry Company. Mr. KNOWLES said be thought that was so and he be. Heved that if the Newton Authority as well as their OWn Authority were to approach the Vulcan Foundry Com- pany they would no doubt do something to improve it. The SURVEYOR said the traffic had increased very much indeed lately,on that road. A portion of it was a private road belonging to the Vulcan Foundry Company. They (the Highway Authority) had repaired a portion of it at the other end. Mr. KNOWLES suggested that Mr. Thompson should see the Surveyor to the Newton Authority to talk over the matter, and if thought advisable to see Mr. Coiling- wo )cl of the Vulcan Foundry Company who, he said,was a most enterprising man. The SURVEYOR said the road at the Earlestown end was very good up to a certain point. Mr. KNowtEs said the brook bridge, and the London and North-Western Railway bridge were not wide enough and wer ivery dangerous. He had had two narrow escapes of having a smash there. If the Vulcan Foundry Com- pany would assist the two local authoritiel in widening the bridge and improving the road, he thought it would be of great benefit to the district. Mr. THORNTON thought the representatives for the district connected with the Council ought to have a voice in such a matter. Mr. KNOWLES: Tao Councillor for the district is not here. Mr. THORNTON considered that it was a great mistake when the recommendations of the representatives for the districts were choked. Mr. KNOWLES said he did not know that they had been choked. Mr. THORNTON contended that they haCi, ani that it was a discouragement to the par!sh councils when their recommendations were not attended to. Mr. KNOWLES: Some parish councils are apt to go further than the Rural District Council is inclined to go. Mr. THORNTON: They have a right to be consulted. After further discussion it was decided that the Sur- veyor meet the Surveyor of the Newton Council to talk the matter over and that the subject should be further considered by a future meeting.

BREAKFAST AND SUPPER.

THE PROTECrIVE POTENCY.

Why do cows and horses so generally avoid the poison- ous herbs which grow in pastures? '‘lnstinct," you say. What is that? It is a word far somethin -4. nobody under- stands. Lucky, though, for the dumb beaita that they have it. Bar the trait called " instinct " and the poor creatures would be at tie mercy of luck and their appetites.

And it's right along this line that we come in—we human animals, I mean. If there was nothing to stop us doing just what we should like, where do you fancy most of us would fetch up? A five barred gate across the road is a fearful nuisarsce whea a fellow is in a break-n3ok hurry ; bu b3tter that than a spill into a washout just on the furtaer side of it.

Here,on this old pine table of mine, are a lot of letters —all from people who were pretty bad in years agone, but are well now. Thank the Lord for the latter fact, say I, with a sincere upward roll of my eyes ; and they are a heap more thankful for themselves than lam for them.

The pint I want to bring out—and I do seem to be a tidy while at it—is this ; that in nearly all those letters I spoke of the writers say one thing with verbal varia- tions. and say it sadly, plaintively, as if it was one of the most dangerous and melancholy: experiences recor- ded outside of Fox's Book of Martyrs.

" My appetite was poor ;" " I had little or no ap- petite ;" "I had lost all relish for food ;" " I sat at the table, but couldn't swallow a morsel ;" " they tried to tempt me with dainty dishes,but it was no use—l couldn't stand even a taste of tlae best of thenr" And so forth and so on; all these dear goad souls complaining over lhe loss of love for eating, as they might lament the loss of a life's savings through the customary " inexplica- ble defalcation of this trusted officer of the bank." But was the cessation of appnite under the circum- stances a loss at all ? No, I say. it was a fortunate and providential occurrence ; it was the five-barred gate that kept thorn from tumbling pelt-melt into the wash- out, or into an excavation in the parish churchyard— which comes to the same thing. Hear a witness or two :

"For years," testifies one, " I have been a grea sufferer from indiges:ioa aad waikne is. I was ne v properly well—always weak and low. I had no relish for toed, and at ter meals (scanty and light though they were) I had weight and pain at my chest and back. None of the mediciaes I took helped me a particle. At length I read about how Mother Seigel's syrup had cured persons suffering in this way, and started to try it. The first bottle relieved me ; my app 3tite soon ret Hued, and my ability t 3 digest food corresponded with my enjoyment of it. A little later and the ailment was driven away, and I was stronger and healthier than I bad been since I was first attacked long years.before. For so great a deliver- ance I have reason to appreciate the merits of Mother Seigel's Syrnp—and I do." (Signed):(Mrs.) MARY ANN Woop, 91, Salisbury-itreet, Ilkeston-road, Nottingham, March 1, 1899.

" I can hardly remember," testifies another, " when It was that I began to suffer from indigestion. My story is, of c3ur3e, the same as that of thousands. My appetite fell off and remained feeble Mid poor ; after eating I always had I:distress and pain. Then I com- menced to realise that the disease was telling up on me

I got so weak I could hirdly cceep about. At this time I first heard of Maher Seigel' s Syrup, and began to ueo

it. Tnis remedy quickly relieved me, and hi a reasonable time worked a complete cure. What I think of it and how heartily I commend it you may infer from this fact. I now have a good appetite, and can satisfy it without fear of bad resume." (Signed) J. D. WILLIAMS' Marazion, near Paeztnce, March 23, 1899.

What would happen, think you, if a keen appetit walked hand in hand with a torpid digestion? Turn over the idea of that deadly combinat ion in your mind, No ; when the millstones get clogged, Natu re will let yoia pour no more corn in the hopper. She shuts off the appetite till she can make repairs. And to make them the tool she uses is Mother Seigel's Syrup.

OUR. LOCA.L CHURCHES.

WORKINGMEN'S MISSION._ BANK QUAY..

The harvest thanksgiving services were held in the above Mission on Sunday, when Mr. Collier, of Lowton delivered two eloqueit discourses. A special anthem "The earth is the Lord's"was ably rendered by the choir. There were crowded congregations, and the collecions which were in aid of the trust funds realized a satisfac- tory amount. The building was most tastefully decor- ated with flowers, fruit, and cereals, by the lady friends f the mission.

"FURIOUS DRIVING." This was the subject of the Rev. W. Bracecamp's ad- dress at the weekly meeting of the Men's Bible Class, held in S*. Peter's Church. on Sunday afternoon. The many cases of furious driving heard before the local magistrates recently, he said, had suggested the subj,ct, and he wished to apply the spiritual lessons to be derived from its consideration. He based his remarks on the wcrds " For he (Jehu) driveth furiously " (11. Kings ix. 20). There were many men to-day driving furiously with regard to the evils of drink, gambling, immorality and other sins, and he urged upon his hearers the neces- sity of curbing up before their sins brought them to disaster. Miss Lily Jowett sang two solos, " The Vesper Hymn" and "i The Holy City." in both of which she was heard to advantage. Mr. Webster was at the organ.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A. SOCIETY. REFERENCE TO CURRENT TOPICS BY MR. WALLINGTON.

There was more than an average attendance at th 3 meeting of the P.S.A. Society held in Wycliffe Church on Sunday afteinoon. Mr. George Wallington presided and in the course of an interesting address he referred to the inscription over a house in Chester bearing the words " God's providence is mine inheritance." He said these words were a source of comfort to an inhabitant of Chester two hundred years ago during the plague whe most of the inhabitants fled. He proceeded to refer to the great comfort to be derived from them and coin- mended them te the acceptance of his hearers. During the past few cleys he had been very anxious as to what would be the outcome of certain events. One of them was the diffieulty in the Transvaal. He believed there was not the slightest cause for war. (Hear, hear.) He was one of those who struggle 1 for the franchise in this country in 1868 and he was in favour of every man on his becoming twenty-one years of age, having a vote without any trouble. He believed that the franchise could be obtained in the Transvaal without the shedding of blood. (Hear, bear.) He hoped that Go] who held all men in his hands would powerfully move thTs people that right might be done and that people might know that they were not going to be governed and ruled by men whose fear was their money bags. The whele secret of the difficulty was this, tut if there had been tie gold in the Transvaal, Kruger and his people wou'd 'nave been left alone. Our chartered company who were reiponsible for the Jameson raid were trying to force us into war, , bat we must it t them know that we were not in favour lof it. The best newspapers in the country were in favour of a peaceful solution and it was only a few stockbrokers and shareholders in the money market who wanted war. He could not sit down without referring to another matter which ' had occurred just across the channel. Ho confessed he was confounded when he heard Dreyfus had been found guilty and sentenced to ten years imprisonment. His blood bailed, and he was sure their blood boiled also when they read it. He had read that case very carefully since it had come up for revision and he would not hang a cat on the evidence. There had been ne real evidence at all. They might depend upon it that the people of France were blind. The generals of France were knavee, and the ministers of France were as bad. God pity that country that could be in the hands of such fools and knaves. The sentence was an outrage on everything that was right an:l just. There was an old phrase" The end is not yet" and he believed that was so in coenec- don with this case. His sympathies had always been with France, acd he had been of the opinion that the Republic would have been on the side of that which was right. His faith had lren very largely shaken, and if the present position of aff airs in France was the outlome of Republicanism, God help poor France. " God's providence is mine inheritance" and God took an interest in all matters in this world, whether international or local matters. He could not refrain from saying one other thing. The characters of our prominent local mesa were of Some concern to them. They wanted respectable men to cen- duct their local affairs. (Hear, hear.) A. gentleman had said to him " Don't you think Alderman Roberts was wrong in saying what he did ?'' He replied that he dei not think so. When a person got up and charged Alder- man Roberts with taking whisky a greater insult cou'd not have been offered him. It would have been right enough if Dr. Cannel' had said it of so no people, but when he made a charge of that sort against. a man who had been a life abstainer and a man of public standing, he was perfecay justifi,d in telling him it was an infamous lie. Let the members of that Society make it known that they were aaxiou 3 to have men on the council of good character, and that they would remember them at the proper time. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Wood, of Warrington then delivered a most interesting Bible address on the lite of Achan. A nom- her of new members were admitted, the membership for the new year exceeding five hundred.

BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CFEURCII. INTERESTING SERMON BY TEIE REV. H. G. ROBERTS.

Preaching at the Bold-street W,sleyan Church on Sun- day evening, tahine• for his subject " The thorn in the flesh," the Rev. H. G. Roberts said that the exposition of the thorn in the flesh was most interesting and valu- able, but it had toe n buried under such a mass Of con- jecture that the simple lessons it contained were hard to reach. The main object had been to discover what the exact nature of the thorn wig. This strife was typical of much sticly of the Bible to-lay, infinite scrutiny of the form without much thought of the end. The preacher referred,to the various theories which had been advanced. When referring to the theory that it was some eternal trial the opposition of the Judazing party , —a party characterised by mali,T,a party that slandered the apostle—he said that some of these characteristics belonged not to one age hut were with vis to-day.

_ THE DISORACEEUL PROCEEDINGS AT THE TOWN COUNCIL MEETING

Alluding to the disgraceful proceedings at the Town Cgiuncil meetinrf, the rev, gentleman sail he was no: traducing the character of our public meta, for many were as distinguished for nobility of character and for their exemplary lives, as for their ability and eloquence. surely the time must COMP, here,in this town when they should make higher demands upon the loyalty of some of their representatives and officials tp the great principals of morality and righteousness which lay at the foundation of their common wealth. He pleaded for a higher ideal of citizenship which was the only thing that would raise the standard of the public life of the town.

THE DREYFUS CASE.

The Sp.aker also referred to the Dreyfus cis.. He said that St. Paul's earnestness was regardel with sus- picion and condemned. They sneered at it. All the great leaders of the world had been in earnest. Our civil and religious liberty we owed to the enthusiasm of great leaders. John Knox laid the foundation of the liberty of Scotland ; John Wesley revived the religious life of England. It Was the enthusiasm of Garabaldi. Cavours and Mazzini that created a new Italy, and would that Franc. at this critical moment had such mon to control the fierce passions of a wild democracy and unscrupulous aristocracy. God bless Dreyfus and his noble wife. They prayed that Franc 3 might spring on the wings of a better wisdom. CHRISTIAN SCIENTISTS.

IsThe rev. gentleman also made allusions to the new rr- ligion the religion of Christian science, and characteriz d it as a mixture of religion and blasphemy, a combina- tion of impudence awl imolk humlitF, of ignorance and religiosity, and a conglomeration of pseudoscience and bastard philosophy.

A HINT FOR THR TOWN COUNCIL,

The preacher closed by showing the differepcebetween the Christian view of suffering, and the `Niew of the stoic. Socrates went astray on that question ; they were solemnly told that he married Xanthippe although aware of her fearful temper. He de- liberately married her in order to learn patience. He affirtned that if he could endure the sneers, criticism, and scorn of his wife evory dav,he would be able to keep himself in check when misrepresented and abused in debate. This mei.hod proved effective, and he (Mr. Roterts) strongly recommended that discipline to some of our public men. In Socrates they saw one of the most cifted men who ever trod this world, mistake the mission of suffering in human life. Suffering was a transfigured thing for the apostle—he auffered fur the sake of something divine.

ORDER OF DRUIDS.

MERSEY LODGE. WARRINGTON DISTRICT.

A meeting of the above lodge was held at the Bridge Dm on Saturday, for the purpose of presenting P. A. Joseph Oxley with a certificate of merit of the Order, framed in a handsome oak frame, for his servids to the lodge as M.G.A. since its inception nine months ago. The certificate had been illuminated by Bro. Pooloiy,aed was much admired. Bro. D. Houghton, D.O S., pre- sided, and made the presentation on behalf of the lodge. He was sure it would be pleasing to the recipi- ent to be surrounded by so many friends and well-- wishers, which of itself was sufficient to convey to him the esteem and regard of all. Bro. Oxley would pass on that day to a higher degree of their order, and in future would be entit:ed to Past Arch—a tide they considered he had well earned, and would worthily maintain. That mark of distinction,together with that beautiful emblem, were evidences of the good-will and appreciation of his fellow members, for the marked ability he had us- payed in the discharge of his duties as N.G.A. of the lodge. It was a pleasure to him (the chairman) to testify to the faithful and zealous manner in which the work of the lodge had been carrisd on, under the guidance and direction of their past Noble Grand Arch, and ever since he had been ass )ciated with their district he had, by his kindly manner, won the good-will and esteem of his fellow members. (Applause.) The lodge had not only been a success both numerically and financially, but had set an example to older ones to recognize what could be achieved by energy and per- , severance. It was well known that Bro. Oxley had taken pains to instruct members in the principles of the society, endeavouring at all timin to inculcate a spirit of unity, securing the co-operation of each member in furthering the interests of the lodge. He, therefore, on behalf of the members, asked him to receive that pre- sentation from his fellow members, with their best wishes that he might be spared for many years to look upon it as a tokel of their regard and appreci atico-- Bro. Pooley endorsed the remarks of the chairman, and thanked the members for their invit Ltion to be present, giving him that opportunity to wituess how highly they had appreciated Bro. Oxley's services during his tenure of office. He felt proud of the part he took in the open- ing of that lodge and in assisting in the initiation of Bro. Oxley as a member, which was now increased by seeing him raised to the degree of Past Grand Arch— In rising to reply, Bro. Oxley, who was deeply effected said he was greatly rejoiced to think that what he had attempted to do had met with approval, but be was sure they had to generously recognised his services: He hoped ta be spared to do more in the future. He, therefore, begged to thank them, and would prize the presmitatiin as one of the most valuable articles he had. The members and friends then went for a drive into the country, and being favoured with fine weather, the out was much enjoyed. Tea was partaken of on the route, and a game of b )wls indulged in. Returning to the lodge room a smoker was held, members vieing with each 'other in contributing to the harmony of the evening by vocal and instrumental music. A vote of thanks was accorded to the D.C.S. for making the presentation and presiding over the me(ting.

INQUEST AND VERDICT. Mr. S. Brighouse,Ncounty coroner, held an inquest at the Warrington Police Station, on Tuesday after- noon, on the body of Mary Ann lioarty, a girl of 19, who was recovered from the Nfersey on londav afternoon.—Michael 1-I.oarty, 85, Sharp-street. said

deceased was his daughter, and was 19 years o-f-a-g; last birthday. She was a factory operative. On Sunday he was working, and she brought him his dinner at noon. He never saw her alive again. He had never seen her do anything that would cause him

to suspect that she would commit suicide. She had stayed out all night before, and he had cautioned her about it. He could not say where she was on Sunday night. THE movement to boy Should we boycott the French Exhibition? cott the Paris Exposition Universelle of next year on account of the judicial crime perpetrated at Rennes, appears to be spreading, alike in thia country, on the Continent, and in America. 1310 no official sanction is likely to be given to the movement in any Continental country, or even iø America, and it is being strongly discouraged ill influential circles in Germany. The actions of private individuals or companies, of course, cannot be controlled. But we do not think the movement is one that the English people generally should favour, and we are not surprised to find that there is a reaction setting in against it among the more intelligent and thoughtful of our countrymen. T° boycott the ExhiLition would be at once unjust to France, injurious to ourselves, and calculated to defeat its object. If the " boy" cott " be intended as a p,tnishment it wotdd punish the innocent as much as the guilty. France is not " a nation of scoundrels," as one irresponsible writer called her, just because a military and clerical clique has, in this matter, obtained temporarily the upper hand. She has many righteous men, who protest as strongly aS we against the prostitution of justice. She is the country of LABOR! and PICQUART, as well as of MERciER. and Du CLAM. The powerful appeal for a " fifth act" in the drama—an act of repara" tion—which M. Zola has addressed to the " Aurore," will strike a responsive chord among thousands of Frenchmen. To condemn a whole nation for the crime of a few would be both injus- tice and folly. Then again, for us to red fuse to exhibit at Paris, while Germany and sol3e other countries are making lavish display of the products of their industries, would really only ill' jure ourselves, by giving a further impetus to the vogue of articles "made in Germany." We cair not cut off our trade with France, or isolate ha commercially, though we might very seriouslal injure our own commerce by attempts 0 do so. Indeed, just so far as we damaged French trade should we injure our owri by depriving ourselves to that extent of a customer. Moreover, any such boycott would really defeat its own ends. Instead of helping DREYFUS and promoting the triumph of justice, lt would only exasperate France and play the game of the anti-Dreyfusards. They would be abie to poiat to the boycott as nroof that the movement in favour of DREYFUS is of foreign origin and hoS" tile to France. The French "reptile Press," te whose efforts in throwing dust in the eyes of th.o people, the strong anti-Dreyfusite prejudice le mainly due, would be provided with fresh political capital to carry on its nefarious crusade. More over, the French blinistry would suffer from the failure of the Exposition of next year. M. WAS" DECK-ROUSSEAU'S Cabinet has done much to sear! justice for DREYFUS and to break down the tary ring. That Ministry has, in a measure, stake its existence on the success of the Exhibition, allu anything which strikes a blow at this strikes at the Government, and, what is even worse, places weapon in the hands of the enemies of the RePillr. lie. The anti-Dreyfusards are anti-Republic The dynastic pretenders and military clique woula profit most by the failure of the Exposition:, Englishmen who desire"f air-play for the Republic, will, therefore, seek some better method than .010 boycott to convince the French people of the wine and the blunder committed in its name.

IN one of his pleasan° cnat and Iron. social talks last week, Lora ROSEBERY made reference tO the probable exhaustion of our coalfields. Coal' raising is still our chief industry, and a plain source of our national wealth. But the question whether our coal stores are likely to run short is beginning to pass from an academic and sPsen_i lative to a serious and practical stage. lA' ROSEBERY seemed to think we were somewhat tO. prodigal with our coal, seeing that We were raisir.g over two hundred millions of tons Pef annum. The statesman has yet to arise who Wil seriously propose to levy an export duty on coot to check the "lavish and uncontrolled" destruction of our mineral capital. But the time rag 11° be far distant when such a drastic rasa-sure would become a necessity to enable us t° "husband ': our shrinking resources. Als3ll with coal, iron is usually coupled—coal au° iron being, paradoxical as the statement mai seem, the two main factors in making Britain the .. land of tin," in the modern sense. Tne fears of a coal famine refer to a more or less remote future but we have already come appreciably near to ea ron famine.
