ittTNCORN NOTES.

[By A CORRESPONDENT.I At the meetings of the Board of Guardians and the Rural District Council last Tuesday, the esti- mates for the ensuing half year were submitted. The Guardians recommended a call of 7d. rate,w'nich was the same as lust half year ; but the Rural Dis- trict Council have reduced their rate by id. in the .E. There was nothing extraordinary contemplated, and on the whole the estimates are subjects for congratulation.

At length, the Liberal Club—which for so many years has been the only political club the town pos- sessed—is threatened with opposition in the shape of a proposed Constitutional Club. I believe that the new club is going to be conducted by a kind of Limited Liability Company and will naturally rely mostly upon its shareholders for support—a means by which a double interest is secured. The actvent of the " opposition "will, no doubt,do something to forward the alterations contemplated by the com- mittee of the Liberal Club ; so that instead of hav- ing to rely upon the present scarcely adequate ac- commodation in the way of resorts the town will shortly have two fully equipped political clubs, to cater for its requirements

I should certainly like to call—if I may be al- lowed to—certain individuals, who have a persis- tent disregard for anything in the shape of stand- ing orders, to order! It may be convenient for some members of the Rural District Council, and certain of its officials, to remain seated whenever they consider they should take part in the Councils deliberations but to other members present, and especiallytto:their chairman,it is,to say the least,dis- respectful. Added to nis, in a crowded room, it causes a deal of inconvenience to press men, and especially so in a room where the accommodation for the Press is accommodation only in name.

Dr. Adams (the Medical Officer of Health for the Runc3rn Union), appears to take a very arbitrary course respecting the closing of the Helsby Schools and notwithstanding the testimony of several of the most influential people in the Parish of kielsby seems determined to suit his own convenience in the matter of granting the certificate. His argument appears to be, that, so far, no one qufficiently qualified to determine what is measles and what is not measles has given him information concrete enough upon which to go. Which may be true enough ; still the most generous course the Dr. could have adopted would be to ascertain the facts himself and proceed ac- cordingly. Very little time and trouble spent thus, would have 133 en conducive to a better feeling all round. I don't say the doctor stuck out for any- thing but " duty," but I do say, there are times when there's a call for more than "duty."

Mr. Samuel Taylor (the new president of the Runcorn Football Club) proved quite a capable footballer, and showed his capabilities last Saturday when be played in his first match. In some measure perhaps, Broughton were intimidated, for to play 15 men and a President—and that president a " Sammy" too—is sufficient to strike terror into any combination. The augury is a happy one when such sympathy is seen between a club, its president and its supporters. May it continue so.

What a crowd of cdnsolers will gather around— yea, veritable Job's comforters—when you loße half-a-crown down a street grid. Such was the proceedings of a gentleman proceeding down High- street, on Wednesday morning last. But that gentleman possessed business acumen of no mean order, und forthwith bargained with a roadms.n to restore the half-crown on commission. The work commenced, and quite a crowd soon collected to watch the operation, so that 23 6d had caused losses to employers of labour (especially of errand boys) ; to the District Council, vide roadman and tools, and to numerous others, of a few hundred per cent. more than its value. How true it is " misfortune s never happen singly."

RUNCORN MASTERS OF THE SITUATION.

BY THE "SCORCHER."

The cupholders—Broughton Rangers abou whom I will confess I was afraid to say anythin g last week, were on Saturday last fairly "crimped-up" on the Irwell-lane enclosure. Brough- ton'A backs tried to stem the torrent but all in vain. Toeir supporters cried "a James! a Jam3s l" But, as if in derision there resounded from the partisans of:Runcorn " a Jimmy 1 a Jimmy !" Then followed a crash, and James met Jimmy with but one result —that Jimmy won. It would not at all do for me to describe the game, as a game; for, to be para- doxical it" were no game.' It was a display—that was all,just a display, in which the Redtails showed their capabilities to their followers. As soon as the battle commenced Runcorn were sure of winning; when it was half-time Runcorn had won; but just about three quarter time Broughton Rangers were "snuffed out." They all looked as if a bath and a rub down would suit them better than " fac- ing the music" for that last quarter. As for the locals they seemed to be as ready to go on when the and came as when the start took place; and, when he " cease" resounded, they were complete masters of the situation' and Broughton had to acknowledge it too. Of Broughton, I wilt say but this; they have a great reputation to sus- tain and endeavoured to sustain it. They had class in their ranks and no mean metal But though they were the Broughton of old! What could they do against the spirit of the Run- corn of old? Nothing! And sadly, yet sorrowfully they seemed to realise that at length a mightier than they have arisen—to whom they must give place. Thus they retired avowedly beaten upon their merits. What of the units that make up the Runcorn Fifteen? They were almost perfect, but still they must train and make tbems-lves capable of maintaining that speed, strength,grit, and endur- ance, which SO able reduced the redoutable Rangers into a mere" pulp," Once again! must remind them that on Saturday (to-morrow) they meet Widnes, and I look to them for the performance of the "hat-trick"—the winning of three home matches in successlon. I want Run- corn to win, a regular knock-out win; add feel con- fident they will if they follow the "Scorcher's " advice and keep the" pot boiling" all the way.

RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

This Council held its usual fortnightly meeting at the Workhouse. Dutton, last Tuesday. There were present: Messrs J Knowles (chairman), J Mosley Williams, J Mclntyre, Joseph BalFhaw, E Clarke. C E Linaker, Thomas Lewis, T Wright, H Ma,wdsley, J Taylor, T Darlington, S Woodward, G Pilling, P Gleave, E Bretherton, J Spruce, W Fietcher, P L-ather, E Wall- ington, J B Chadwick, T Antwiss G F Ashton (clerk), W E Hough (assistant clerk), Ames Farrington (in- spector), w Diggle and J Swinton (surveyors), and Dr J Adams (medical officer.) THE SMOKE NUISANCE AT WESTON.

Mr. W. Mortimer, of Beaconhurst, Frocisham, wrote to Mr. Ashton, the clerk, as follows :—" Dear Sirs—l shall be glad to know if you, as clerk to the Runcorn Union, can do anything towards stopping the smoke nuisance from the Castner-Kellner chimneys. The smoke from them is very dense and black, and is making all the vegetation about here very black. If you can du anything towards stopping it, I shall feel greatly obliged. Mr. Mosley Williams said he could confirm that com- plaint. They could have no idea of the density of the smoke which blew over Frodsham from those chimneys at Weston, and if it had not been for the remarks made by Mr. Chadwick at the last meeting, he was for propos- ing that some steps be taken. But if Mr. Chadwick was still satisfied that they were going on with their esperi- ments,he would be content to leavelit for a while longer. Mr. Chadwick thought it would do no harm to write to the Castoer-Keliner Company about the smoke. The Chairman We shall certainly have to reply to Mr. Mortimer, therefore I suggest that the Clerk write to the Costner Knellner Company, enclosing a copy of the letter from Mr. Mortimer, and acquaint that gentle- man of the fact. Mr. Mosley Williams suggested they should also add other complaints which had been received by the Council. Subsequently the two suggestions iwere unanimously adopted.

The assessable value of the district as per the valuation lists in force was £153,144. Mr. Chadwick seconded arid the Council unanimously assented. THE POLLUTION OF' RIVERS.—IS THE CANAL A RIVER? Dr. Adams in submitting his monthly report, referred to the enclosure of a copy of a 'letter from the Clerk to the County Council, which he bad received from the Clerk to the Rural District Council, in which it was inti- mated that the County Council were glad to find that the District Council was taking action in the matter of the Rivera Pollution. and further requesting particulars of the action the District Council were taking. So as to be able to reply to that query, he bad forwarded a re- quest to Mr. Farrington (the inspector), asking him to take samnlee of certain effluents at different points, and he (Mr. Farrington) had replied that the duty did not belong to his office. He (the medical officer) supposed that Mr. Farrington had reasons of his own for not doing so. The only point in Mr. Farrington's letter he was desirous of getting explained, was the clause where he said" It does not belong to my department." He consi- dered that as medical officer he had more or less a stand- ing order to instruct the inspector to take samples from streams. But he was of the opinion that on this occa- sion he svas carrying out the request of the Council that samples should be taken. Mr. Farrington said it was clear to him that the whole question was one of whether the Ship Canal was less sanitary now than it used to be? and to that, there was only one answer; it was not as pure as it used to be. Speaking at some considerable length he said that the Doctor had forwarded to him a request and information where be would like to have samples of effluents taken from, but at qach place there was no effluent at all. Notwithstanding the fact that it was not his duty to have anything to de with sewers—that was a matter for the surveyors—he had brought for the Council to see, samples of the Ship Canal water taken about three years agolat Halton Gutter,Saltport,below Latchford Locks and above Latchford Locks, and by looking at them anyone who had seen the water of the Ship Canal lately could see the difference. A long discussion followed in which Mr. Wallingtoe inquired whether they were talking

about the Ship Canal-or the River Mersey. Mr. Mclntyre said that did not seem to him to be the question at all it was simply a question of whose duty it was te take samples as requested by the doctor. Mr. Farrington persisted that it was not his duty, but if the Council requested him to do it he would. That was another matter. The Clerk in answer to Dr. Adams stated that if the effluents spoken of were in streams it was certainly a matter tor the inspector, but if they were sewers it was as certain a matter for the surveyors. The only question was whether they were streams or sewers. Mr. Farrington contended they were regarded as sewers. The question being raised respecting the pollution of the Mersey by the sewerage of Stockton Heath, Mr. Farrington pointed out that the eewerage of Stockton Heath did not belong to the Council. it was the Warrington Corporation to whom it belonged. They took it over and if there was anyttmag wrong with the effluent of their sewer they would have to rectify it. It was no question for the Council. Several members concurred with the Inspector. The Medical officer anxious to be clear on the legal basis, asked that the Clerk should procure the agree- ment between the Council and the Corporation for the nest meeting. The Clerk replied that he would. Subsequently, on the motion of Mr. T. Wright, seconded by Mr. J. Mclntyre, it was decided that samples of the Ship Canal water should be taken at the points, as had been done 2 years previously,and that the same Committee be appointed to superintend the taking of the scmples with the substitution of Mr. C. E. Linaker for the late Mr. Haywood, and the Clerk for the late Clerk ; and also that Mr. Farrington's offer to take the samples be accepted. Mr. Mclntyre also added a rider which was agreed to, that in the meantime steps should be taken to decide whose duty it was to take the samples.

THR CLOSING OF HELSBY SCHOOLS -A DILEMMA.

Mr. Taylor (Helsby) called the attention of the Coun- cil to the position of the managers of the Schoqls at FreMy. He had a letter that morning from the Vicar of Helsby, stating that 99 children were absent from school through the measles,and appealing that something should be done an they (the managers) could not get a certificate from the medical officor to close the schools. Dr. Adams, in reply to several members said his posi- tion was peculiar in that he would have to furnish the Local Government Board with all particulars if he granted the certificate. Those particulars he had not got, and besides it was not competent that a School- master or a School correspondent should state that the children were away from school through the measles. He wanted the names and addresses of the people sup- posed to be down with measles, then he could have something to work upon. It was pointed out that those particulars could be had if a post-lard had been dropped to the school mana- gers. Dr. Adam; stated that no such particulars had been given him when he saw the vicar personally. Mr. Linaker pointed out that the vicar of the place bad written to Dr. Adams twice about the affair and re- ceived no answer. What were the managers to do? If the school was not closed there would be a further spread of the disease, besides a loss to the managers and if they closed them without the certificate they would be liable to loose the Education Department Grants. Dr. Adams entended that had he the information to

work upon he could have gone,as in the case of Stockton H3atb, and taken every fifth house and visited it, and if he found that measles did prevail there he would have granted the certificate. He did not think that ten of the people were under medical treatment. Mr. Taylor said that the Dr. would find that the majority of the cases were being medically treated. After further discussion the matter dropped. PREVALENCE: OF DIARRLICEA AT HALTON, _ _

Mr. T. Wright having spoken of the high percentage of deaths in Halton due to diarrhces, said that some suspicion had been entertained Gf the water supply. He therefore requested that samples should be drawn off the mains for analysis. This was agreed to.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

iron has been discussed in the iron and steel trades with some degree of lively apprehension. Pig-iron has rapidly increased in price, and stocks have run low. The very active demand for .the commodity which has marked the trade boom of the present year, on both sides of the Atlantic,has,temporarily at least, overtaken the supply. The statistics just published by the British Iron Trade Association, however, are somewhat reassuring. During the first six months of the present year the output of pig-iron in this country increased by about 350,000 tons, raising the quantity produced in six months to 4,78'2,868 tons. But only some two-thirds of the blast furnaces of the United Kingdom have been blast; and although of the remaining third a arge number are antiquated and not adapted to modern methods, the production of " pig " could soon be increased to at least ten million tons per year. But the difficllty lies in regard to available sources of the raw material. Great Britain is not now producing anything like the quantity of iron- ore that she did formerly. In 1880 over eighteen millions of tons were raised. Last year, although the amount was in excess of several previous years' outputs, the quantity was only fourteen millions. This country no longer stands at the head of the world as an iron producer. She is second only to America, with Germany creeping up rapidly as a gaol third. There are, however, new iron-ore mines being opened in various parts of the North of England, and new discoveries Rf ore deposits have been made in Cornwall and Devon, and even, like coat, in Kent. Whether these new iron fields will serve to ward off the predicted iron famine, and enable Great Britain to maintain her indus- trial predominance, is at present problematical.

How many people who look at the one picture will see within it the lines of the other ? Mr. Cornelius Vanderbilt, American millionaire,owner of lordly palaces, head of railway trusts, church- giver, and philanthropist—in a word, a colossal though a modest figure in an important country. Mr. Van der Bilt, Dutch settler in the States, industrous, rather niggardly, religious, but some- vlat dour, a little coarse in manners, and a little ~.00 opinionated to make a pleasant travelling companion for a long journey. Rub out the frugality a no longer needed, let the industry re- main, for the millionaire, as long as health lasted, was a great worker, give religion the sunshine of prosperity, cut away the corners, and smooth and polish by a liberal education, and strengthen the opinions with the authority and interesting impressiveness of twenty-two carat gold, and you admire the one man just as you shrug your shoulders over the other. The portraits are those of the distinguished American citizen who has just passed away, and of his an- cestor, the founder of the Vanderbilt fortunes. The qualities that make a celebrity are not always attractive, but when he is made, when they are gilded over, then they are highly esteemed. Mr. Vanderbilt's firmness was displayed in his renunci- ation of his son Cornelius, when the latter made a marriage that, in the father's eyes, seemed unsuit- able, and his hereditary sense of duty was proved in a thousand and one private benevolences, of which the world never heard collectively. His last illness was probably aggravated by his riches. After his first attack of paralysis, all that science could do to avert a fatal return was done, and these numerous precautions and constant watchfulness probably tended to make thc . patient more nervous, and therefore more liable than he would have been under ordinary circumstances.

In an obscure German paper which, however, has the merit of being strongly Dreyfusard, the formation is advocated of a" Society of Monte Cristo." The meritorious league is to walk in the steps of the romantic Count, and thus to com- pass the punishment of Mercier, Roget, de Beaure- paire, and the other conspirators. All that is needed, we are told, is plenty of money and a band of daring adventu-ers. We should have expected a project of this kind to have seen the light in the office of some wild American paper, That it should be seriously mooted by Germans shows how deeply the verdict has been taken to heart, and how hot is the indignation against those who procured Dreyfus's condemna- tion. We are not informed what punish- ment the Society of Monte Cristo would inflict upon the abducted offenders. Presumably the torture inflicted by Dumas upon the banker would be considered heavy enough. The popular British officer who declared the other day that no dangerous and romantic adventures are possible nowadays, would find his fill of adventure as a member of the suggested society. Stranger things have happened. We do not seriously expect any German to respond to the appeal of the German editor. Still the idea is a brilliant one from the point of view of the sensation-loving " yellow " press of America. One of those journals did actually suggest to a member of the staff that he should take command of an expedition for the rescue of Dreyfus from Devil's Isle. The abduc- tion of Dreyfus s persecutors would he easier, and would have the merit of providing a longer story. That German journalist should take his idea to America.

There is a new illuminant coming into vogue. We have already got coal gas, acetylene gas, elec- tric light, and petroleum in constant use and now there is another method of dispelling darkness de- vised. Alcohol is the new illuminant—carburated alcohol—which is looked upon as an ideal light as it is odourless' clean and safe. The German Em- peror and the President of France have both made use of it; consequently it is finding some favour on the Continent. Probably it will be welcomed here. At any rate we could find numbers of people who would throw themselves heart and soul into the burning of all the alcohol in the country. It should have a future before it as an illuminant and heating agent in a land where there is so much said and clone for temperance reform. Good Tem- plar Lodges and Temperance 'Societies of all kinds ought, without exception, to consume alcohol in this manner. They will then be doing much for their cause, for alcohol which has been utilised for lighting and heating a room _cannot possibly make any poor soul drunk. But, joking apart, there is much to be said in its favour. In the United States of America, where waste molasses have to be burnt at an actual expense for fear of creating a nuisance, it would be possible to distil industrial alcohol from the waste material. The farmer with his millions of bushels of unsold Indian corn would also find a profitable outlet for his surplusage. France professes to see nothing less than her agri- cultural salvation in the illuminant, provided the Government will remove the burdensome repres- sive laws against private distilleries. Then, too, when there is so much said about our coal supply giving out, the advantages of alcohol in its newlrole cannot fail to be appreciated.

Temperance in Warrington is a cause which is enthusiastically supported by a limited few but does not make any very striking headway. It has much to contend with and the struggle against intemperance is here an exceedingly severe one, but that does not discourage the workers by any means. At Latchford there has been a very healthy temperance movement for over half a cen- tury, and of which Councillor Ellison has been the central figure for the whole of that time. Here too, difficulties have had to be encountered—the society at one time was housed in a barn—but they have been overcome by pluck and persever- ance of a high order, and now the local lodge meets in a suitable hall, the debt on which has been almost cleared off with a sale held at the end of last week. The Good Tomplars of Latchford have never won fame or notoriety, but they have pursued their worthy aim with an earnestness and zeal which do them infinite credit, and which have made Latchford a better place to live in. Many a man and woman in the district owes his moral and physical salva- tion to the labours of his brothers of the Bonehill Branch Lodge, and the ministrations in particular

lof its esteemed president, Councillor Luke Ellison. Working unostentatiously as they do,the members of the Lodge are not blessed with overmuch out- side support, but they have never relaxed their endeavours in the slightest, and probably never will. Theirs is a labour which needs no reward other than that vouchsafed them by good results and we cordially wish them success that will glad- den their hearts.

The abnormal increase in the death rate of War- rington during the past few weeks should give the Health Committee and the members of the Town Council generally grave cause to inquire into the circumstances which have led to such a regret- table state of affairs. The facts as presented in the Medical Officer's return for the week ending Saturday September 2nd are serious, if not alarm- ing. The number of deaths during the week was 57, and actually exceeded the number of births by 5. The death rate works out at 42.2 per 1,000 against 27.3 in the corresponding period of last year. Diarrhoeal diseases accounted for 36 deaths which, we should think, is the highest point touched. The return relating to the Fever Hospital is also of serious import. On the first day of this month there were no fewer than 53 cases in the hospital and at the pre- sent moment the accommodation is taxed to the uttermost. The question naturally arises " What is the cause ?" We believe Dr. Gorman, the Medical Officer, has partially if not completely answered this question himself. "War- rington is full of filth" is the remark which fell from his lips at the Borough Police Court on Fri- day, and although the statement may not be strictly accurate, those who have made even.a cursory inspection of the courts, yards, and alleys of the town must have often marvelled at the supineness of the authorities in dealing with such prolific sources of disease.

ACCIDENT AT WIDNES. Sam Johnson, 120,Warrington-road, a fireman engaged at the Silver and Copper Works, Widnes, whilst engaged at his work was scalded on his left side and leg, due to the bursting of a pipe. He was removed to the Acci- dent Hospital, and was attended to by Dr. Eatock. ANOTHER FIRE AT GLOBE. CHEMICAL WORICS. On Thursday morning, another fire, the third in a few months, occurred at the Globe Chemical Works, Widnes. resulting in the total destruction of the chlorate plant and shed. The outbreak was caused by oil used in the process boiling over on to the furnace, and this caused the leaden condenser to burst, scattering the burning chlorate of sulphur, and setting fire to the shed. The fire brigade were summoned, and.preveated the flames spreading. The damage was considerable. ST. LUKE'S MISSION. RUNCORN. At the Widnes Petty Sessions, on Thursday morning, before the Mayor, Alderman Waring, and Mr. Timmis, John Yarwood, a fittmeu, living on the flat Rachel, was charged by the London and North.Weetern Railway Company with contravening section 123 of the regula- tions of that Sankey and St. Helens Casa' by obstructing the entrance to the lock and refusing to withdraw his vessel, thus setting the officials at defiance. —Walter Sandbach, lockmaster at the Widnes loeks, said that on Angust 23 at half-past one he filled the lock to allow the fiat Harmony to pass, which was loaded with acid, to go into the river Defendant pieced hie flat in such a position across the lock that nothing could be done. He asked him to move astern and allow the boat to pass, but this he refused to do, After con- siderable persuasion he moved out of the way, and the 1 Harmony passed into the river. Defendant's vessel was light. The lock was again filled to allow another flat to pass which was also loaded. Defeniant placed !his flat in a similar position, and absolutely declined to move. The wisest course they could adopt was to let him pass into the river. A delay ef ten minutes was caused, and the flat ran great risk of losing a day's tide. Defendant, who was light, could have entered the river an hour later. When he got into the river he anchored, and did not go lout until the morning tide.—After further evidence, Yarwood was fined 40s and costs, or one month's im- prisonment. .

A MOKTH FOR NEGLECTING HIS FAMILY. At the Runcorn Police Court, on Friday, before Mr. F. J. Norman and Mr. J. Ockleston, Thomas Johnson, chemical latourer, was brought up on a warrant, at the instance of John Fredshana, collector for the Guardians of the Runcorn Union, charged with having on the 15th August, at Dutton. wilfully neglected to maintain his family of three children, whereby they had become chargeable to the common fund of the union.—From the evidence it appeared that prisoner was committed to prison by the Runcorn Bench for four months' hard labour for neglecting his children in a manner likely to cause them unnecessary suffering or injury to health. At that time the prisoner's three children were taken to the workhouse, and when his sentence expired he never made the slightest inquiry with regard to them, and they had still remained at the House, at a cost to the Guardians of £l.O 1.3.5. 63. The Guardians wished ta make an example of the prisoner.—Prisoner pleaded guilty. He said he had injured his wrist at work, but he promised to refund the money if the Bench would deal leniently with him.—The magistrates sent him to gaol for one month's hard labour. WIDNES CYCLE CLUB CHURCII PARADE. The Widnes Cycle Club. last Sunday held a successful Church Parade and established what was quite an in- bovation by attending morning service at St. Paul's Church. There was a good mustar of cyclists,about 60, nearly all members of the local club, accompanied by a few visitors from Runcorn. The cyclists were received at the Town Hail, at 10 a.m., by the Mayor (Alderman S. W. Wareing), and as the hour of service approached the company moved to the appaintect place of wnrship, the cycles being stored in the Town Hall yard. The ser- vice was conduc'ed by the Vicar (Rev. C. E. Stocks, M.A.), his Worship the Mayor reading the lesions. An excellent discourse was given by the Vicar from the text, " In all thy ways acknowledge Him, and He shall direct thy paths." and was listened to with great attention. The rev, gentleman applied his sutiject to all departments of life, both public and private. Referring to the question of Sunday cycling he deprecated it if it interfered with the duty of public worship. Mr. W. Cooke presided at the organ, and the choir rendered an anthem in an able manner. At the close of the service, and at the invitation of the Mayor, the cyclists repaired to the parlour at the Town Hall, where his Worship ex- pressed the hope that they had all enj -Ted the service, to which remark an unanimous assent was given.

SPIRITUALIST MEETINGS, On Sunday afternoon, at the Spiritualist Hall, Bewsey-street, instead of the usual address by the engaged speaker,the President read an extract from the St. James' "Parish Mitgazine." This was fol- lowed by clairvoyance by Miss Smith. In the evening Miss Smith gave an address on " Death, and what after," conclading with a number of clairvoyant delineations. On Monday the whole evening, after an opening hymn, was devoted to psychometry, when the lady Wl3 successful in almost every in- stance, in some cases recalling events that occurred forty and fifty years ago.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The usual fortnightly meeting of the above Board was held at the Workhouse, Dutton, last Tuesday, Mr. J. Mosley-Williams (presiding). There were also present:— Messrs H Taylor,A R Norman, S Woodward, T Reynolds Dr Gastaldi, the Rural District Councillors, with Mr G F Ashton (clerk), Mr W E Hough (assistant clerk). and Mr James Cunnah (master).

THE LATE M. PICKERING, OF NORLEY,

Before the commencement of the business of the Board the Chairman referred to the loss the Beard and Council had sustained, through the death of Mr. William Pickering, the representative for Norley. Mr. Pickering had represented that district for an interrupted period of about 20 years, and all present could testify to the pleasant intercourse they had always had with Mr. eickering. Having referred feelingly to his own intimate acquaintance with the late Mr. Pickering, the Chairman proposed that the Clerk be instructed to for- ward a letter of condolence to the sorrowing family ex- pressing the sympathies of the Council. Mr. J. Mclntyre seconded, and the vote was unani- mously passed in silence.

===== 0002601_18990915_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LOANS.-BETTER TERMS.

Letters were received from Messrs. E. 0. Preston and Co.. 4, Tokenhouse Buildings, London, and the Public Works Loan Board respec`ing the proposed loan of £1,500 required by the Guardians to meet the Expenses incurred by the alteration of the Board room and the Tramp Wards. Messrs. Preston and Co. asked 3± per cent. for 20 year3,and a charge of £2l for procuring the loan. The Public Works Loan Board intimated that the rate of interest atlwhich they were at present empowered to grant loans on security of local rates repayable in 20 years was 21 per cent per annum.—lt was decided to negotiate with the Public Works Loan Board for the money. APPOINTMENT OF CERTAIN SUBORDINATE_ OFFICERS..

The Local Government Board forwarded two copies of a general order dealing with the appointment of : Collector of the Guardians, collector of poor rates,school attendance officer, inquiry officer, porter, assistant teachers, " Industrial Trainer," superintendent of out- door labour. The object of the order was to obviate the Board of Guardians reporting such appointments to the Lccal Government Board for confirmation.—The order, however, in no way affected the salaries of the officers named.

FINANCE COMMITTEE.

Mr. Woodward submitted the financial statement, which showed out relief paid as follows :—Runcorn £B5; Budworth, £24 ; Frodsham, £27. The balance at the last meeting was £3,137 43 4d ; cheques drawn this day £3,704 93 61, leaving an adverse balance of £27 11s. Mr. Wright seconded the adoptioh of the report which was agree to.

THE ILLNESS OF DR. CARRITTHERS : SYMPATHY FROM THE GUARDIANS.

The Clerk read the following letter from Dr. Carruthers :—Halton, Runcorn, September 11th, Dear Sir,—l am still laid up suffering from the effects of the accident which have turned out more seriously than at first anticipated. I ant daily improving, but as it will be some time before lam able to resume my duties, I wish to ask the Guardians permission tor three months leave of absence, and fAr Dr. Morris my locum temenc to act as Medical Officer and public vaccinator while lam incapacitated. Mr. T. Wright who proposed that the necessary leave of absence be granted Dr. Carruthars, said he had seen the doctor who was in bed and unable to get up. The Doctor (Dr. Morris) who came in hit place was a very nice fellow and one he was sure quite capable of dis- charging the duties whilst Dr. Carruthers was absent. He thought that under the circumstances Dr. Carruthers should be allowed the three months asked for.

Mr. J. Mclntyre seconded, and s 3veral guardians hay- in expressed sympathy with Dr. Carruthers, it was unanimously agreed to.

THE RELIEF SUPERVISION COMMITTEE.-.-MR. ROYLANCE ANSWERED.

The Chairman observed that at the Relief Supervision C-mrt that morning, Mr. John Frodsham, the collector, had drawn attention to the remarks made by Mr. Roy- lance at a previous meeting of the Board respecting the very high percentage of uncollected amounts which were outstanding. He (the chairman) wished to say that Mr. John Frodsham had submitted an explanation and shown to the satisfaction of the committee that the amount of contributions for maintenance of relatives was considerably less than it had been in previous years. Toe Committee had expressed satisfaction with Mr. Frodsham's explanation, and he the Chairman was only sorry that Mr. Roylance was not present to receive it. However be had noticed Mr. Gleave (who was Mr. Roylance's co-representative) present, and he had ex- pressed satisfaction at the explanation. Mr. S. Woodward stated, in addition to Mr. Williams remarks, Mr. Frodsham had pointed out that when h first took office, the amount due to the Board, then out- standing, was £440. whilst at the present moment the total amount recoverable was something like Subsequently a vote expressing satisfaction with Mr. Frodsbam's explanation was passed.

THE ESTIMATES.-AN UNCHANGED RATE.

Mr. S. Woodward submitted the estimates of the con- tributions required from the various parishes in the Union for the half-year ending Lady Day, 1900, which was calculated on the assessable value per valuation lists in force, and moved their adoption. The total capital for the current half year, ending Michaelmas, 1899, amoun- ted to £9,242, and the net probable expenditure was estimated at £7.626, which showed a balance at Michaelmas of £1,616. The probable expenditure during thaensuing half year, as set out in the detailed estimate, was made up aq follows : Comma charges, £2170 ; county rate, £3425 ; halance and contingency fund. £2170 ;or a total of £7765, which after deducting the probable balance in favour of the Parishes at Michael- mas, 1899. left £6149 revired from the overseers. The assessable value of the union as per valuation lists in force was £208,426. A rate of 7d in the £ on the assess- able value of the union would produce the sum of £6,080. and it was me-man:leaded that a call for the sum of £6,149 (being equivalent to the call made for the last half year)be made upon the overseers in accordance with the detailed esti ma tes.after deducting the estimated balances in favour of each parish at Michaelmas 1899. Mr. Wrrght seconded and the estimates were agreed to.

PAUPER LUNATICS,-A SLIGHT INCREASE.

Mr. A. R. Norman reported that the deputation, ap- pointed at the last Council meeting to visit the pauper lunatics confined in the County Asylum, at Upton, and chargeable to the Runcorn Union, had visited the asylum. They found that the number of paupers charge- able to the Runcorn Union was 3 more than the previous year, being 65 compared with 62. During the twelve months eleven had been admitted, and discharged and there was one death. Whilst being sorry to say that the balance was on the wrong side, it was still very gratify- ing to know, that whilst some were coming in others were being discharged, and the doctor of the institution had expressed the hope that in a short time others would be discharged. The deputation were delighted with the place, and everything they saw there, had the appeal,- ance of being applied so as to afford every facility for the reborn of the afil cted ones to their right minds. Mr. Piling anti Mr. Reynolds the other members of the deputation endorsed Mr. Norman's remarks. The report was adopted. THE ROUSE AFFAIRS.

Mr. James Cunnah, the Master, in submitting his re- port, sated the children spent A delightful day at Rhyl on the 6th of September, and though this year they had been afforded two outings instead of one, there was a balance standing to the credit of the treat fund. (Hear, hear.) He had to report that the Industrial trainers (male and female) had applied for testimonials, as they were desirous of finding another place. He could only speak very highly of them during the time they had been in the house, and hoped the Board would grant the testi- monials asked. The Chairman added in connection with the treat to the children, he wished to say he had accompanied the children, and a most delightful day was spent. He wished also to put on record the kind hospitality of Mr. Taylor and the Vicar of Frodsham whom the Guardians had met there. Besides kindly receiving them, Mr. Taylor had been esnecially generous to the children and and provided them all with a hugh stick of toffee. (Hear, hear.) Mr. A. R. Norman considering it would he impossible to thank each one who had contributed to the fund for the children's treat individually, proposed a vote of thanks be accorded, embracing all who had contributed to the enjoyment of the children in the huuse. The Chairman in seconding the vote of thanks to the donors, asked that the vote of thanks also include the Master and Matron and the other officers who had done so much to look after the children. Ha was sure that had those who contributed to the fund seen the children or been with them at Rhyl, they would have been sure that their money WAS neither ill-spent or ill-enjoyed: (Bear, hear.) . . . .

Mr. Norman intimated he would include that. The vote was unanimously carried. Mr. Cunnah returned thsnks briefly. On the motion of Ms. J. Mclntyre it was unanimously decided to grant the testimonials to the industrial trainers.

WORKINGAIEN'S MISSION BANK QUAY.

TEA pA.itTI AND CONCERT IN TIIE NEW SCEIOOLS.

The new schools in coanection with the Working- men's Mission, Bank Qaay, so generously presented to the neighbourhood by Councillor John Crosfield have now been in tale for about a fortnight, and to celebrate their opening a tea party and concert were held in them on Monday evening. At 6 o'clock, about two hundred sat down to tea, and ample justice was done te a mot excellent repast. The following ladies presided at the tables :—Mesdames George Dale, Thomas Dale, Edward Wallington, Joseph Burndred, George Hassell, R Turner,Thomas Ashley, T Taylor, Birch, J S Monks, Josenh Monks, junior, A Rough, Joseph Taylor, and Ball. At a quarter Co eight an entertainc'ent was given, Mr. R. A. Naylor,who was accompanied by Miss Naylor, occupying the chair. There was an exceptionally large attendance, the building being filled to its Utmost capacizy. In opening the proceedings, The CaaiintaN, who was cordially received, said that in the first place he wished to teank them for their hearty reception, and in the sscoed place to congratu- late them on the fact that they had taken possession of those buildings provided through the munificence oE the ' benefactor, Councillor Crosfield. lie had been to many gatherings similar to the preseet one, in the old schoolroom, and had always found that the programme their friends had arranged was of it very high class character. Ther - had been nothing of a low or vulgar character, which could only appeal to the degraded twos of humanity. It had always been of a very elevating class, and he believe] the friends in that new building would carry out the idea that had prevailed in the past, that they would do nothing to drag down, but do something to lift up those who attended those gatherings. Some people thought we were not a musical nation, and that we did not care for music. He had been to a great many entertainments in different parts of the country,and had noticed that whenever there had tren a piece of music of a high-elass character, it had been mivh appreciated A song of an elevated teedencv always receive] the heartiest round of applause. We might not be so far advanced as some of our continental neighbours, hut we meat not forget that in the past, Eegland had produced some very fine musicians. To hundred years ago an English musician died, and was buried in Westminster Abbey. He referred tri the man of whom they were all proud as Englishmen—Henry Purcell. (Applauee). He was a musical genius and could compose the most beauti- ful aathems when a mere boy. As he advanced in years he produced some magnificent pieces of music, some of which were sung in the churches of this countryto day. He did not liva to be an old man for he died in the prime of manhood. It was recorded that he was once Olin- manded by Mary,the Consort of King William. to Appear before her at a certain house she was visiting. He then played the laarpsicord and the Queen asked hinito per- form a Scottish ballad called "Cold and Raw," and that piece so irr tated him because it was of a vulgar character that he never forgot it, and when commanded to compose a birthday ode in the year 1692 he performed what in the musical world would be considered a won- derful feat. He arranged to introduce the Sco'ch bal- lad alluded to, the air to form the bass part of the beautiful piece of music which he produced and which was sung as a birthday ode in honour of her Majesty. That he (Mr. Naylor) considered a most difficult task but Purcell was only one of the many musical geniuses produced by England. Re was proed to know that the musical education of the rising generation was not beine neglected. He thought people were growing fonder of music every day, for music had its charms and was said to have an effect even on the savage beast.. (Laugh- ter and applause.) An excellent programme was then anbmitted, the artistes being Mr John Watson and Miss Mabel Watson, of Burnley, society entertainers, Miss Lena Dobson, of Warrington (contralto), and Miss R Attwood. A.L.C.M., of Crewe (silo pianist and accompanis`). All the Reim were mest credi.lably rendered. Mr. Watson scared a gre at success with Jerome's .ketch " How Uncle Podger hurl.' the Picture," which caused roars of laughter. His other contributions " The old man goes to town," and " How we laugh," were also well received. Miss Watson was deservedly applauded for her rendition of the song " Good-night, pretty stars," for the sineine of which she was awarded a gold medal at Manchester in 1896. Mr. and Miss Watson also sang a couple of humorous duets entitled " The Naegletons" and " A.8.C." Miss Attwood, of Crewe gave two pianoforte solos with taste and skill,and the audience were no stinting in their ap- plause. She also discharged the &Ales of accompanist. Miss Lena Dobson pessessen a sweet contralto voice, which was beard to advantage in the songs "Kathleen Mavourneen," and " By the Fountain," for the latter of which she was vigourensly encored and in reeponse feelingly sang "The Old Folks at Home." attending the concert. He said they came with great readiness to any of their meetings to which they were invited. He was informed that Mr. N tylor bad two

BEFORE the MAGISTRATES'

SHOCKING PARENTAL NEGLECT AT WARRINGTON. At the Warrin4ton Police Court on Saturday, before Mr. T. Birchall and ether magistrates, James and Eden Hennighan were charged on a warrant with neglecting their four children in a manner likely to cause them nnneceepary suffering and injury to. health.—Mr. A. Browne, who prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C. said that prisoners formerly lived in Winwick-road. The man was a labourer earning 183 2d a week; his wife was employed in a marine store shop and earned 53 and a son 83. Inspector Hackett on the 27th May visited the house and found that both father and mother wer. away, pre- sumably at work, but the family were in a terrible con- dition. The children were fairly well nourished, but they were badly clothed, and their clothing was also filthy and dirty, indeed they looked more like street arabs than anything else. A summons was issued against the parents, but they did not appear, and it was ascertained that they had gone to Manchester, and that being the case it was de- cided to take no further action against them while thay remained in that city. Their neglect in Mancheeter.how- ever, came under the notice of the atrhorities, and a re- presentation was made to Inspector Eiackett, at War- riugton, with the result that the prisoners were appre- hended and brought here. The children were sent to the Manchester Shelter, and they would be handed over to the Goardians, where they would be better looked after. Their ages were twelve, seven, five, and four years. Mr- Brown described the condition of the children, which was disgraceful, and askrd the Bench to deal with the prisoners severely.— The Chairman: No wonder there is a spread of disease 'under such circumstances.—Inspector Hack -tt coal- borated.—_-William Jones, chief school attendanco officer, said the statements which had been made to the Bench were perfectly correct. It was in couseqeence of rep- resentations made to him that be communicated with InspecVir Hackett.—Dr. Bowden said that in his opin• ion the room in which he first saw the children was un- fit for hum in habitation. The child Dania was suffer- ing from a bad eye, the result of celd and dirt.—Evid- ewe having been given by the Manchester inspector, who stated that the prisoners had been living in an attic in Angel-me ado w for which they paid 73. a week (furnished).— The Chief Constable observed that the male prisoner had been before the Bench 18 times, principally for neglecting to send the ctildren 0 school.—The Chair- man (addressing the prisoners) said : 'We have care- fully considered this case. The very fact that you have neglected your children's education shows that you are utterly careless. But that is not the question we have to deal with now. Such peaple as you are a danger to the community, and such dirt cannot exist in a place without spreading disease more or less. You cannot be allowed to treat your children like pigs. You have fed them, there is that to be said for you, but you have taken no care of their health or comfort. We have decided to send you both to gaol for two months with hard labour.

A crowd of six thousand spectates-3 greeted the War- rington and Leigh teams as they stepped into the en- clo3ure at Wilderspoli en Saturday. This was the first match of the s,ason for the premier team os their native heath, and there were many anxious to see the new " captures" and take stock of the capabilities of the team as a wh:le. When it became known that neither of the new men—Jones and Merry weather—were going to turn out, and that in addition, Mlrrison was unab:e to play, the prospects of the home team winning against the powerful Leigh combination were very dismal. TIRE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, SEPTEMBER, 15, 1899.

THE TEMPERANCE CAUSE AT LATCH- FORD.

EXIIIBITION AND SALE.

A debt of £l5O hoe hung over the temperance Hall at Latchford for a number of years, and this has been a source of anxiety to all connected with it. Furthermore, the need has been felt for larger premises, but the workers did not feel justified in increasing the accommodation until the old debt had been removed. It was with the inteation of accomplishing this object that an exhibition and sale of plants, flowers, fruit, vegetables, and useful and fancy articles was held last Thu;:day,and again on Friday, in the Temperance Hall. The accommo- dation of the little room was taxed to the utmost capacity, but it proved equal to the exigencies of the occasion, and it is pleasing to note that the anticipations of the promoters were realised. The whole of the articles offered for sale had been kindly given, and the proceeds will therefore be devoted in their entirety to the object in view. There was an excellent collection of plants and flowers, and these were chiefly the gifts of Mr. J. C. Parr, Mr. William Long, Mr. Fletcher and Mrs. Broadbent, Numerous articles of wearing apparel as well as a number of fancy goods were offered for sale, and one stall was almost entirely occupied by such a requieite household commodity as soap. Then in addition there was a confused medley of things at a stall rtihtly called the jumble stall Toe sale would, of course, have been incomplete without al refreshment stall, and this department was in the capable hands of a number of ladies. 1 The opening ceremony commenced punctually at three o'clock, and the ceremony was gracefully performed by Miss Naylor. Amongst those also present were Mr. R. A. Naylor, Councillor Ellison, Mr. B. Few, Mr. W. 11. Ford, Mr. Arthur Ford, Nlr. Blease Mr Isaac Brundrit, Mr. Ctiffe, and a large number of ladies from the immediate neighbour- hood. Councillor William Pierpoint, J.P., had in- tended to be present, but was unavoidably preven- ted from doing so. The proceedings opened with the singing of a hymn and prayer. Couneillor ELLISON, prior to calling upon Miss Naylor to open th- sale, said he. thought it might be a fitting op- portunity at the c immencenaent of their proceedings to give a very brief outline of the work which had been carried on in Latchford in the Tempr ante cause. He might say in the first place th at they commenced their work in the year 1873, that was 25 years ago, at Christ Church, Latchford. They continued their work there for one year, ha-, in the following year they undertook to renovate an old barn whicn then stood on tile present site of the Wealevam Church. Here the work was con- tinued until 1879 when the present building in which they were assembled was erected at a cost of about £4OO Of this sum £250 was raised and a debt of £l5O re- mained. He regretted to say that up to the present time they had not been in a position to make any seious effort to clear off the debt owing to other efforts and the long continuation of bad trade. They believed the time had now come wh.tn an effort shauld he made. A com- mittee was formed and they determined if possible to carry out a sale and exhibition to a successful issue. Sa far they had met with fair success. They had had pro- mises from various gentlemen in the neighbourhood amounting up to the present to about £2O. They had also promises from their own members of a like sum which brought the total up to £4O. They were trusting that afternoon and oa the following ctay they would be able to augment that to a consider- able extent. He hoped all present had come with full purses and that thee' would go away with them empty. (Laughter.) It would be a benefit to the Temperance Movement, but perhaps not to them. He was sure they were all pleased to see Miss Naylor pre- sent. She was a lady whom they all reverence and re- spected for the good work she had done and was doing at the present time in the Temperance cause, and they hoped and prayed she would lone be spared to continue her generous and noble efforts. (Applause.) Miss NAYLOR in declaring the sale of work open said she was very pleased to be there that afternoon. She thought they bad met to perform a very good work. She heard that morning that the sale had teen visited by Canon Davies so she therefore supposed it had now been consecrated. (Laughter.) She hoped everything would pass off very satisfactorily and that they might have a successful sale. She would be very pleased to help them in her small way. She hoped the Temperance Cause in Latchford would flourish and that they might have a good number of adherents. She would give them as a motto, " Beloved, imitate not that which is evil, but that which is good." (Applause.) Mr. FEW proposed a vote of thanks to Miss Naylor and referred to the valuable work which was being done by Miss Naylor and her family with the Temperance cause. Her brother was doing a noble work in connection with the Warrington and District Band of Hope Union; They were also aware that he conducted temperance work in connection with the Temperance Hall at Thelwall. They owed a great debt of gratitude ta them for coming to such a humble place as their Temperance Hall at Latch- ford. (Applause.) The vote was carried with acclamation. -- - - -

_ Mr. NAYLOR said he was requested on behalf of his sister to thank them for the hearty aote of thanks they had presented to her on that occasion. Mr. Few had said the Temperance Hall was a very humble sort of place. He would rather come amongst humble people and to a humble place than to a large place. Perhaps it was a peculiarity of temperament that one could not change. However, it was a great p'easure to come into that building, one with which they had been associated many years age, and it was gratifying to know that in the interval a gm it work had been done and the hall had become a centre of usefulness. He hoped they would put forth renewed efforts for the extention of the work. An old proverb said that" Heaven helped those who helped themselves " and it was perfectly true. In canclusion he thanked them for the kind vote of thanks and hoped the sale of work would exceed their antici- pations. (Applause.) The stallholdera were as follow : Refreshment Stall.—Mrs. D Ellism, Mrs Walker, Mrs Pearson, Miss Fairhurst. Stall No. I,—Mrs Few, Mrs A Hampton, Miss E Fletcher. Stall No, 2.—Mrs W H Ford, Mrs Henry Blease. Stall No. 3.—Mies Fletcher, Miss Foster, Miss Peers. Stall No; 4.—Miss Weeks, Miss Nellie Hampton, Mr F Blease and Miss Pearson. _ _ _

Flower Stall.—Mr. E G:eave, Mr B Few, Mr Henry Blease. Jumble Stall.—Miss Wick. FRIDAY'S PROCEEDINGS.

There was a fair attendance at the opening of the sale on Friday, the ceremony being performed by Mrs. James Fairelough, the ex-Mas cress. Amongst those present were Mrs Henry Roberts, Mrs Ellison, Miss Nellie Fair- dough, Mrs Few, the Rev J F Matthews, Councillor Ellison, and Mr Ford. After a hymn had been sung and the Rev. J. F. Matthews had offered prayer Councillor Emeson said that before they commenced the proceedings he must apologise for the absence of Alderman'lloberta who had promised to act as chairman. He was glad to say he had sent his" better half" to take his place, He was sure they were all pleased to see her. Before he asked her to take up part of her duty, he might report the amounts that had been received and promised. £4O had been promised and given. The receipts on Thursday amounted to £25 10s, making a total of £65 10a., with which they were all highly satisfied. The priz for bouquet.; arranged by children had been awarded a; follows ;-1. Arthur Clarke ; 2, Clara Forater ; 3, Lily Lamb ; 4, Alice Forster. de would now ask Mrs. Roberta and Mrs. Fairclough to come forward and go through the opening proceedings. (Applause). MTS. H. ROBERTS who was c.rdiallv received said she was sorry her presence was somewhat of a disappoint- ment to them. Her husband had overlooked an impor- tant committee meeting when he promised to be present. It was such a pleasant duty to introduce Mrs. Feirclough that she had taken his place willingly. If she were speaking to strangers she might tell them how worthy and lovable Mrs. Fiairclough was, but they knew how ready she always was to give help. She had now great pleasure in introducing Mrs. Fairclough and asking her to open the sale. (Applause.) Mrs. FAIRCLOCTGH slid she thought the motto of those who attended a bastaar ought to be "to do good by their action." She was at a littie sale a fortnight ago in a village in Cum herland, and the opening ceremony was se• long and tedious that she thought they would nave t ) go away with- out spending a penny. She hoped the heart; and the management would be gladdened by a handsome increase in the amount obtained, and wished the baz tar

every success, and that it would be an impetus to the promgters for further efforts in the future. She had now pleasure in dtclaring the exhibition open. (Applause.) Mr. ARTHUR FORD proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. Fairclough on behalf of the Temperance Society. The Rev. J. F. MATTHEWS seconded, and the motion was carried unanimously. Councillor ELLisox proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. Roberts. and it was carried with acclamation. - _

Mrs. FaincLitroa said she had to apolDgiv, for the ab3ence ot her husband, as he had to attend th.. 3 same meeting as Alderman Roberts. The sale was then proceeded with. The proceeds cf Friday's sales, with subscriptions, amounted to £l5, making a tptal of £BO.

?Laws to the Otittor.

Authors of of letters intended for publication must in all cases qend their names and addresses to the Editor. latter a intended for publication must b 3 written on one side only of the paper. Correspondenta are strongly advised to write briefly. A short letter has much greater chance of insertion than a long one.

THE SCENE AT THE COUNCIL MEETING. Sir.—The peculiar and effectual methods by which the Warrington Corporation secured an overwhelming vote for their great Bill, and the unseemly and deplorable spectacle presented in tbe Council Chamber on the 5t inst. are surely indications that the government of War- rington is open to improvement. For a long time the opinion has prevailed in many quarters, that our muni- cipal affairs are being managed by a small and rather exclusive clique who regard their partners in the Council as El) many machines for voting to order. And worse Still, it was openly alleged at the meeting referred to that the financial operations of the Council were, to some ex- tent at least, carried on under a cloak. Nor was this serious charge made without book. Indeed the Chair- man of the Fitiance Committee himself supported it by reading a resolution passed no longer ago than the 28th of August last te the effect" That in the opinion of this Committee (Finance), it is not expedient to give infor- mation to individual ratepayers, and requests that the particulars be given by the Chairman at the next Council meeting." Now I think there can be no difference of opinion as to the unjust and dangerous character of such a principle. The Corporation and its officials, whatever airs they may assume, are not masters of the situation, but only the servants of the ratepayers. And whoever heard of ser- vants or employes daring to refuse to render an account of their stewardship! Such refusal would at once arouse suspicion, and might result in dismissal. The ratepayers who contribute all the funds of the Corporation have a moral and, I should think, a legal right to know when and how every penny of their money is expended. To pass resolutions, then, to block or retard their way to obtain such information is to court suspicion, and when suspicion is excited there is an end of confidence. As Dr. Cannell and Alderman Bolton, as members of the Council, must know a great deal more of the inner workings of that body than the outside public can pos- sibly do we are bound to give attention to their aver- ments unless it can be shown that their statemonts were reckless and unfounded. When they deliberately charged the Council, or part of it, with secret dealings with public money, and individual members with presenting bills for services in connection with the water scheme the honour of the Council was seriously menaced. And it seems to me that thc obvious duty of the ratepayers under such circumstances is to demand an investigation to verify or disprove such allegations. It may be said that all disbursements made by the Corpora- tion must pass under the -eyes of the local auditors. I assume that this is true. But tee duty of these auditors is, I understand, mainly confined to passing the vouchers placed before them by the Corporation officials, without power to disallow any account so submitted. I have long held the opinion that the monetary transactions of all boroughs should be audited by independent Government officials authorised to reject and surcharge all illegal payments. Such audits would satisy the ratepayers that their money was expended for legitimate purposes only, and also prevent the occurrence of such miserable scenes as the one wit- nessed in our Council/on tke sth inst.—Yours truly. lONA. 13th September, 1899.

THE REPORT OF TFIE BOROUGH AUDITORS. Sir,—ln consequence of the abrupt conclusion of the proceedings of the last Council meeting, I was not able to introduce the motion of .whieh I had given notice in reference to the report of the Borough Auditors for the year ended 31st March last. I see, however, that Mr Councillor Shaw Green has communicated to the press the speech which he had intended to deliver on propos- ing the adoption of the minutes of the Finance Commit- tee, and which, as he says, would probably have obviated the necessity for the motion, as he gives the report in full. lam only sorry that it should have been necessary for me to give notice of the motion at all but, as the report was not published in the ordinary way, and as I was not allowed to read it at the previous Cautcil meeting, I had not any alternative. The final clause (to which the Finance Committee took exception) was insetted after careful consideration, and as the re- eult of certain observations made by the Chairman of the Committee of the House of Commons on the Through Bill, and echoed in the local press. The Chairman appeared to be under the impression that we had some power of surcharge, and as his opinion was evidently widely shared, it seemed te us to be necessary to explain that this was an entire misapprehension ; that, in the words of tbe report, 6` the auditors have no power to enquire into or question the legality of any payment'; and that their duties are confined t the actual verifica- tion of the accounts wh,ch have been sanctioned by the various responsible committees." It will be seen at once that we did not make any charge against the Corporation, and contented our- selves with defining, as concisely as possible, the nature and the limitations of our office. This we felt to b a due alike to ourselves and to the public. The Finance Com- mittee, however, requested us to delete the paragraph altogether, and, as we declined to do se, did n)5 publish the report. No doubt they acted with the best intentions but it 13 obvious that to have recognised their right to modify it, or acquiesced in its suppression, would have been to wholly fail in our duty to the ratepayers. For the moment, the point involved was mainly personal; but the principle at stake was an important one ; more serious issues might at any time arise; and we were unanimously of *pinion that it is better "to perpetuate" what Mr. Councillor Shaw Green deecribes as "a tem- porary and passing matter" than to establish a pre- cedent which might, on some future olcasion, be used with consegnenccs highly detrimental to the town.— Faithfully yours,

===== 0002601_18990915_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
ARTHUR BENNETT, Chartered Accountans., Mayor's Auditor. Warrington, Bth September, 1899.

THE CORPORATION OF WARRINGTON, Sir,—The scenes which took place in the Council Chamber last Tuesday night were unmistakeable evidence of the fact that the government of the town has for some time now teen brought to a low ebb by men who are really unfitted for the positions they occupy. If scenes of such a nature can take place in the Council Chamber, it can be no stretch of itnagitia- tion to believe, that similar if not worse kinds of scenes take place in the Committee Rooms. The natural result of this is that measures and objects which men devote their time and energies to, and, for the benefit of those among whom they live and serve, are hindered and certainly delayed, and it is not at all surprising that the town of Werrington is in a portion of its municipal and social legislation, quite a generation behind the times. As one illustration of thie fact. I need only point out the cendition of' the streets on a Saturday evening,a con- dition which would not be tolerated in any well governed town. The quentlty of drunken men and women who are to be seen about the etreete is simply astounding and gives net only a native but also a straeger to the town when he sees it, the impression, that either the governing 1)14 of the town is weak, or that it is strangely apathetic. The report whichk was famished by the Chief Cotntable and published only a few weeks ago, confirms this view, and to those who take an interest in the social condition of their fellow creatures, the report must have afforded melancholy reading. I know it saddened the hearts of many. We have, I believe, an excellent and intelligent Chief Conetable, who has under his charge a fine looking body of police, and yet they appear powerless to stop the excessive drinking which practically takes place under their own observation. Who are responeible ? The police or the Corporation? Is not the power of control of the police vested in the Corporation ? In reading the report it was significant that not a single convintion against a publican was re- corded during the past year of serving a customer with excessive drink and yet there were hundreds of cases of men and women convicted of being drunk and dieor derly etc. (Drunkenness alone is not considered a crime and therefore thousands are mercifully and justly allowed to go scot free). lam no advocate for the har- assing of publicans. A publican who carries on his -businets in a legitimate manner is entitled to the respect and consideration of his fellow citizens. It is patent, however, that in Warrington the intnxieating liquor laws are being flagrantly and openly disregarded. The remedy le simple. All that ie needed is that those who take upon themselves municipal duties shall perform them. If they are not prepered to do so they are either false to the trust reposed in them or they do not under- stand what municipal duties are and therefore should net undertake them. • In conclusion let me quote the words of Paley, a re- ro2nised authority on governing and with whore words lam in complete accord : "A vigilant meeistracy, an accurate police and an undeviatine impartiality in cerryiny the laws into execution, contribute more to the restraint and suppression of crime than any excessive severity of puniehment."--Yours faithfully. E. ENGLAND.

Moqg Rouge, Rockton TlPatb, 7th SeptembPr, 1899.

VIILCA.N DAy ScnooL.

The children attending this School had their annual treat on Friday afternoon last. The scholars to the number of about 100 were entertained to tea. their wants being attended to by Mesdames Coiling- wood, Carson, Norman. Miss Norman and ethers. After tea theydispersed to the Vulcan Foundry Com- pany's field where they engaged in racing, swinging and other games. As each child left the field foi home they were presented with a bag of nuts and sweets by the Valcan Foundry Company.

PICNIC FROM THE VULCAN. On Saturday the erectors employed ,at the Vulcan Foundry had their second annual picnic. Their destination was Abbey Lakes and thither 27 pro- ceeded in wagonettes. leaving the Vulcan at 12 o'clock, reaching the Abbey Lakes grounds. after a pleasant drive, at 3 o'clock. A bowling handicap was indulged in, and tea, which was of a substan- tial order was served at 4-30. Afterwards dancing, bowling, and boating was indulged in until 7-45 when a start was made for home, the Vulcan being reached at 945. A photograph of the party was taken and each of those accompanying the picnic will be presented with a copy. The arrangements for the outing were efficiently made by Mr. Atkin- son.

ACCIDENT TO MR. R. RITCHIE, NEWTON.

An accident happened to Mr. R Ritchie, of New- ton, on Saturday, at the Home Farm, Liverpool- road, Ashton, and in the occupation of Mr. Richard Hunt; whilst unloading, and erecting an extra large chaff cutter. In doing thi:, he had the assistance of Messrs. Wallace and Tomlinson, and the three suc- ceeded in getting the new machine up two storeys, and when in the act of lowering the old machine, the rope attached to the blocks slipped through the fingers of Mr. Ritchie and one of the assistants. The latter became entangled with the rope, and was thrown to the ground, the fall breaking his right leg. Mr. Ritchie also sustained severe injuries to his head. One of the individuals present had undergone a course of instruction in rendering first aid, and whilst a local doctor was sent for the suf- ferer was undergoing preliminary treatment. The medical gentleman ordered him to his home at New- ton, Dr. Watkins being present when the patient arrived. Mr. Ritchie is now progressing as favour- ably tits may be desired. CLASSES.

The syllabus for the session 1899, 1900, of the classes which are to be held in connection with the above have just been issued to the public. It will be seen from an advertisement in another column, that the classes' open for instruc- tion on Monday evening next, that evening being taken up with Laundry, Shorthand, Book-keeping, Dressmaking and Millinery. There have been one or two slight alterations in the teaching staff. Mr. G. F. Clare who had charge of the Book-keeping classes last session, is replaced by Mr. D. P. Davies of Liverpool, end Mr. T. R. Bradley of Earlestown, replaces Mr. Howorth in the tuition of Grammar, handwriting and composition. The fees are exceptionally low, and there should be no lack of students in the various subjects set down, both scientific and commercial. THE LATE Mn.WILLIAM THOMPSON.

At the quarterly meeting of the Viaduct Insti- tuterechnical Instruction Committee, Earlestown, the following resolution was unanimously passed, and the Secretary was instructed to forward a copy to Mr. William Thompson, Larkfield, Earlestown : --" That it is with very sincere regret we have to record the death of Mr. William Thompson' senr., manager of the Sankey Sugar Company, Earles- town. For a number of years the Company with which the deceased gentleman was so closely con- nected, had by contributions to the prize fund of this Committee, and in other ways, shown its appreciation of, and sympathy with the educa- tional work carried on in this district ; and know- ing that Mr. Thompson took a lively interest in all matters affecting the well-being and educational development, not only of the employes under his own control, but also of the young people in the district generally. this Committee therefore begs to assure the members of: the late Mr. Thompßon's family of its deep sympathy in the great loss they have sustained."—Signed on behalf of the Com- mittee, T. ORMAND, Secretary.--Mr. Thompson, on behalf of the family has acknowledged the receipt of this communication, and thanks the Committee for the kind expressions of sympathy contained in the resolution.

SONS OF TEMPERANCN JUBIL'IN AT RABLESTOWN. On Saturday the members; of the Earlestown Grand Division of the Sons of Temperance Friendly Society, celebrated under vary ausDicions circum- stances the jubilee of their institution. The pro- ceedings included, and were initiated by, the unfcr- ling of the division banner, this ceremony taking place on the Market Square before a large concourse of people. The banner, which has previously been displayed in St. Helens,and is of a beautiful and rich description was unfurled by Bro. W. Davies, M.W. Sentinel of Manchester, and was afterwa' do carried in front of a procession, which paraded the princi- pal streets of Earlestown, headed by the Sutton- road Prize Band. Included in the procession were the Grand Division Officers from St. Halens,Widnes. and Havdock,with representatives from those places and with the members of the Earlestown ledge (Britain's Need), and the Samuel Norley Sectloe of Cadets presented a fairly strong combination, each of the officers wearing their regalia. The proces- sionists dispersed at the Town Hall, and afterward proceeded to " the Co-operat;ve Hall, where an excellent tea was provided and at which labout 400 people were present Later on a concert and public meeting took place in the Town Hall which was well filled. Mr. Charles Harrison, G.W.F. of St. Helens presided and he was supported by Mr. W. Davies, of Vane.helter, and I most of the Grand Division offieers. The pro- Inramme of the concert was sustained by the Cecilia ILadies Choir, under the conductorship of Mrs. Latimer, A.R.C.O. Dining an interval in the musi- cal contributions, emblems were presented to Bros. George Clarke and T Wheatley, for services ren- dered, both having passed through the chairs. ST. HELENS.

ACCIDENT AT A COLLIERY.

John Sullivan, aged sixteen. of Cairn-street, Thatto Reath, wis admitted to the St. Helens Hospital, on Monday. Pufferine from injuries sustained at the Lea Green Colliery. Sullivan was employed among the coal waggons in the pit. and by some means or other wls caught between the waggons and his legs crushed. Dr. Jackson attended him. ATTEMPTED SUICIDE OF A WOMAN.

_ An extraordinary case of attempted suicide occurred at Wilson-street, St. Helens, on Monday night, when a young married woman took a qtrantity of carbolic acid. •The affair happened about five o'clock, at No. 6, Wilson- street, where Mrs. Maud Sparks and her husband lived. These young people are both only eighteen years of age, and they were married six weeks ago. At the time the husband was employed as a labourer at the Pocket Nook Glassworks, but for the past week he has been out of work. Sparks on Monday found his wife in one of the bedrooms in a semi-conscious state. Dr. Dow was immediately called, and after attending to the unfor- tunate woman, he handed her over to the care of the police; who took her in a cab to the Providence Hospital where Dr. Bellew was called in. She is expected to re- cover.

-81;iTE- OF PiUPERISM..IN THE UNION.

_ _ The Rev. W. H. DRUMMOND called the attention of the Board to the printed statemeet of Mr. Jenner Fust, the Local Government Board Inspector, showing the state of pauperism in Lancashire at Lady D ay, 1899. He did not think the report should be received as a mere matter of form. Jo toe first place he would like to call attention to the feet that there was a diminution of pau- pers in the Warrington Union. the figures now reading one in 50 instead of one in 48, but on the other hand, they must not think they were in an enviable position, seeing that th-re were as many as 22 better and only seven worse out of thirty unions in Lancashire. Look ing down the list the cost per head at Warrington seemed considerable, and he would like to kaow whether the increased cost was a general increase over all departments or only of some special department. The average cost of in-maintenance and out-relief during the five years 1894-8 was £8,299. whereas last year it was £9,274, which was praetc Illy an increese of £l,OOO. ACCIDENT TO MR. H. PARKER LOWE.

NO HOPE OF REL;OVERING EYESIGHT. SYMPATHETIC; CORRt4ISPONDENCE Faan

RUNCORN.

It will be remembered that through a shooting accident at Glci,sel, Deeside, Mr. Harry Parker Lowe, a London Barrister, and son-in-law of Sir J. T. Brunner, Bart., M.P., was severely injured and it was feared his sight would be entirely lost. Though attended by eminent Scotch oculists the worst fears have now been realised and it has been stated that the medical gentlemen have no hope of Mr. Lowe ever again being able to see. In connection with thia Rad caiamity we append the following correspondence : Town Hall, Runcorn, 9th September 1899.

Sir John T. Brunner, M.P., Glasse!, K.S.O. Aber- deenshire.—Dear Sir.—lt was with very great re- gret that I heard of the very serious accident which had happened to your son-in-law, Mr. Parker Lowe. I now see by the newspaper account that all hope of saving Mr. Lowe's eyesight has been abandoned. It is such a terrible calamity that I feel that I must express to you and your family my very deep sym- pathy. You have become so closely associated with the life of Runcorn, that what affects you touches us, and lam sure' that the people of this town are greatly concerned at the irreparable loss which has befallen your son. I would again express to you and Lady Brunner, my very deep sympathy with you in your great trouble.—Yours faithfully,

FR A.NCIS WISWALL.

Glassel, R. 5.0., Aberdeenshire, MI Sept. 1899. My dear Mr. Wiswall,—Lady Brunner and I are exceedingly grateful to you for the kind thought which has prompted you to express sympathy with Us in our sad troulYe, on your own account and on account of the town. We are comforted by seeing how bravely poor Mr. Lowe and our daughter bear their great affliction, ant how utterly devoted they are to each other.—With warmest thanks and very kind regards, I remain, yours faithfully, JOHN T. BRUNNER. Francis Wiswall, 2sq.,J.P. CLAIM AGAINST A. FRIENDLY . SOCIETY.
