CASE AT NEWTON SESSIONS.

At Newton Petty Sessions on Thursday before Captain Withington (chairman), and other magistrates, Mary Goan' Queen-etreet, Golborne, claimed £7 163 from Mrs L. E. Bradburn, of Golborne payable on the death of James Faulkner on a policy issued by the Royal London Friendly Society.—Mr. A. E. White, selicitor, Warrington, appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. A. Smith, of Wigan, for the Friendly Society. Mr. White said that he appeared on behalf of Mary Coan who claimed £7 161 from the Royal London Friendly Society, payable on the death of James Faulkner. Complainant's mother was insured in the Society, and on her death the claim WAS paid. Toe person who collected, a man named Bradburn, callFd on plaintiff and asked her if she would allow him to issue a policy on the life of her father' James Faulkner, and offered to see the matter through if she consented. She did consent, and Bradburn went and saw Faulkner, and in due course a policy was issued. He understood that the Society desired to have the COM decided fairly and squarely on its merits. They had no wish to take notice of any technical points. To proceed with the case, Mr White said the policy was canvassed from his client by Bradburn, and Bradburn himself saw the party whose life was to be insured. What took place between them be did not know. Faulkner was an illi- terate man. The premiums were duly paid by plaintiff till December, when she was told that her father had died in September in the Bolt on Workhouse. Faulkner was in the habit of going under two names. He was known sometimes as James Faulkuer and sometimes as James Davies. Being an illiterate man he did not positively know which was his name. The children preferred the name of Faulkner. Bradburn, the collector,had admitted that he knew the man by both names. Mr. Smith said the society did not dispute the man was known by both names. Mr. White, continuing, said that on being informed of her father's ideath, plaintiff obtained a certificate of death from the registrar which stated that James Davies aged 61, died in Bolton Werkhouse on the 16th Septem- ber 1898. The certificate of death was sent to Brad- burn,and he returned it saying that deceased had died in the name of Davies and there would be an awkward- ness in the matter. His client had incurred considerable expense since negotiating with the registrar. After in- quiries it was found that the man entered the workhouse in the name of Davies. On his death the Workhouse Master informed the registrar. When the policy was taken out, his age f was sated as being 54, and when he entered the Workhouse he said he was 61 years of age. Probably he thought he would be ' better treated the older he said he was. His client then went to a great deal of expense to have the certificate altered to the proper age and he produced the corrected certificate which stated that he was 53 years of age. When he submitted the certificate he thought it proved a prima facie case. On the certificate the age was altered to 53 years which Was, however, really younger than that which was given on the policy. That age was obtained—

Mr Sagth.otjected to the source of the age being statpd.

Mr. White said they were prep 'red to say from what source the age was obtained. Cantinuing,he said that the matter had been in consideration for some months, and his client tried with the assistance of her brother to see thy matter throush and obtain the policy. They could not, however, get the society to move in the matter at all. It was then placed in the hands of Mr. Rigg, of Bolton, who had int.rviews with Bradburn. A letter was received on the 17th May from Mr. Fyan, super- intendent at Wigan, but Mr. White contended thtt it did not contain any suggestion of what the society required from the relatives. There was a statement in it, that information had been given to a relative. That relative would be before them and inform them of what took place at an interview he had with Fyan, His client next went to Bolton Wnrkhouse and obtained a certificate showing the date deceased was admitted. The brother took this to Fyan and asked him what more he required, but he absolutely refused to tell him,using the expression that he was not going to give his case away. Some words took placa between them and in the end Fyan ordered him out of the office. The matter was then put into his(Mr.White's)hands as Mr.Rigg did not practicein that court. He sent a letter to Bradburn to ascertain what really were the questions at issue. The reply was that he had already given much time in exolaining the matter to one solicitor, and he did not think at that period he should be disposed to go over the ground with another. He asked them to address their communications in future to the office of Mr. Fyan, Waligate, Wicfan. They considered that the letter should be sent to Mrs. Broadbent, as she was the person to be sued. Mr. Smith thought it was not the proper thing to write to the agent, as the society was the party to pay the amount.

Mr. White said there was no solicitor engaged in the matter at that stage on behalf of the Society, and he was bound to write to the person he was going to sum- mons. Mrs. Coan was perfectly justified in forwarding the letter where she liked. Up to that staee no point really had been raised by the Society, except the general one that they would not pay it. It appeared that Davies was for some time in the Leigh Workhouse, and he was known there by that -name. A man named Stephen Horraghan had knowa him for 14 or 15 rears by both names, and had lived in the same street with him. This man saw him in the Leigh Workhouse in company with a man named Michael Foy, who also knew him by the name of Davies Foyd was in the Bolton Work- house, when he died and actually saw him die. Mr. White read one of the rules of the society showing that all amounts not exceeding £lBO should be paid upon satisfactory proof of the death and name of deceased. and that when required, the local representative should produce the certificate of birth or document or any proof of his correct age to the satisfaction of the committee of management. Mr. White laid particular stress on the words "if required," and said that they absolutely denied refusing having been requited and refusing to produce those proofs, They had been to very much more expense to have the certificate of death altered than it would have cost to obtain the certificate of birth. Braiburn who canvassed the policy and made all -arrangements, expresced himself perfectly satisfied and approached plaintiff with a view to settlement. They refused t take what he offered. He was astonished that the case was brought into court. Mary Coan, Queen-street, Golborne, was then called and gave evidence of having taken out the policy, and the negotiations that bad followed the death of her father. Thomas Faulkner, brother of plaintiff, related his interviews with Mr. Fyan and the Master of Bolton Workhouse, bearing out Mr. White's statement. Themes Horraghan and Michael Foy also gave evidence. They had known deceased for many years, and Foy saw him die in Bolton Workhouse. He went under the name of,James Davies. 'Mr. Smith then addressed the magistrates for the defence. The magistrates unanimou./y decided that the defend- ants should pay the amount claimed together with the full costs and advocates' fee.

DEATH AND FUNERAL OF UR. W. E. WHITTAKER, RUNCORN.

On Monday, the death took place, at Brookfield, Runcorn, of Mr. William Edge Whittaker,one of the oldest and most respected townsmen. The deceased gentleman had been confined to his house for the past six or seven years, and latterly suffered from bronchitis. He had been attended by Dr. Robinson, and although from his advanced age and infirmities death might not have been unexpected, the end came rather suddenly about four o'clock on Monday afternoon. In his better health deceased had served the town in almost every public capacity. He was a member of the old Board of Commissioners, and his shrewdness and business capacity were of great service on the local authority. As a member of the Board of Guardians he undertook work which was very dear to his heart, an 4 not a few will remember with grateful recollection his many acts of charity which characterized his services on the Board. Among the offices he held may be, mentioned his connection with the Burial Board the Savings Bank, the Roncorn Gas Company, and other institutions. He was a staunch Churchman, and for n.,any years held the office of warden. His interest in the Parochial Schools remained unabated up to the time of his death. For years he was a member of the committee of managers, and con- tinued to hold the office of treasurer until his demise. Indeed there are no institutions con- nected wite the Parish Church that have not very largely benefited from his characteristic liberality. The deceased was formerly in business as a chemist and druggist at the old-established premises in High-street now occupied by Mr. J. H. Weston, and it was here that he made a fortune which enabled' him to retire into private life. He filled one or two important offices more immediately connected with the county, not the least important of which was that of a member of the Weaver Trust. Deceased was in his 82n4 year. The funeral took place on Thursday afternoon, and was very largely attended. The cortege formed an imp )sing spectacle as it proceeded from the house to the Parish Church, where the first portion of the service was conducted by the Rev. Canon Maitland Wood, assisted by the Rev. W. H. Stables (Weston.) Re-forming, the mournful procession proceeded to the Greenway-road Cemetery, in the following order :—Teachers and scholars of the Church schools; general public; members of the Urban District Council, School Board, and other public bodies ; Clutch officers • surpliced choir; the Revs Canon Wood, W H Stables, W Scholes, H. B Firth. Eki Oliver and C P Bashforth ; the hearse and body; Doctor's carriages containing: Dr Roberts and Mr A Lake; first coach, Mr W L Clarke, Rev J Eyars (Buxton), and J J W Potter second coach, Mr A Timmins, Mr Smith (brother- in-law), and Nit Walker ; 3rd coach, Mr W T Smith, Mr G Smithand Mr E Imison.—The service throughout was tally choral, and the concluding sentences at the graveside were pronounced hy the Vicar (Rev. Canon Wood.) By the deceased's request there were no flowers, and the funeral arrangements, which were carried out by Mr R Morris, High-street, were of a quiet and unobstru- sive character. Blinds were drawn and other mani- festationsof respect exhibited throughout the town.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RIJI3,BY FOOTBALL. [By ''OyaztsTl History rapeatei I:self on Sturiay. Last yozr, it will be rememhered, Leigh baggeLl two points at the Wilderspool enclosura by ceatainiag a very questionabl try, and on Saturday they succeeded in getting the referee's consent to a try which was still more qusstion able and won the match by it. To score eight points and lose the match aft r all is distinctly hard lines for Warrington. In this connection at any rate I believe Warrington broke a long-staniing record of Leigh's,—and .of which their supporters were istly proud,—at not having had their lines crossed twice in a single match. Warrington may theref ore be ab'e to derive a little consolation from that. But I believe the Warrington score would have been still larger if Jones had been in his place in the three-quarter line. It was decidedly disappointing, perhaps quite as much to the committee as to the spectators, that the match on Saturday had to be played without his assistane in consequence of his not having been able to fulfil the regulations respecting the working ciaule. I think if a special exemption had been applied for in this instance, it would have been ("ranted. Considering the favourable impression which Jones made at St. Helens, there were many anxious to sea him and there is little doubt that it was with the intention of seeing the" new man "that many wended their way to the football enclosure on Saturday.

What can be more disappointing to the spectator, after all this hue and cry about new talent and first- class men, to find that the team chosen to play is a' purely local one P Such was the Case on Saturday with the single exception of Townend. Toe team was really very little different from that whicb was doing duty at the close of last season. This much, however, naay be said in its favour; every man showed greater earnest- ness in his work and a keener desire to win the match than seemed to charac,nrise some of the expositions of last season. I must confess that I have not very great hopes of Warrington reaching the top of the competition ladder this season, and I shall be inclined to be satisfied if they are able to tread one or two steps higher than last season. All the teams in the first division may be considered first-class clubs. Other football committees be- sides Warrington's have been looking for men to improve their combinations, and although none are so fortunate as Runcorn in being able to breed first-class footballers within the precincts of their awn boundary, they have succeeded in bringing in recruits who will add to the keenness in the contest for points.

But to return to Satur 1-ty's match. Leigh were de- layed for some considerable time whilst the London and North-Western Railway Company put on extra coaches to accommodate the large number of Leigh people who were desirous of seeing their unbeaten team make mincemeat of Warrington. It was ten minutes to four o'clock when they made their appearance on the field. In place of the white jersey we were now called upon to admire cerese and white, which has been adopted, so I was told—though it hardiy seems creditable—because the Leigh men succeeded in vanquishing the Batley men in jerseys of the self-same colour. And that reminds me, was it not duly recorded that the Leigh skipper late- sented the Batley captain with this beautifully coloured shirt as a memento of that notable engagement in the first round of the',cup tie?

Saturday's match had not, been in progress many moments before the Leigh men were giving us a taste of their quality. G suley was almost over the line in the first few minutes and Dunbavin and Molyneux were drop-kicking at all angles as though there were nothing else but goals ta score. The pressure of the first few minutes only Eerved to give Bate an opportunity of showing his ability. He led the attack with vigour and behind the "scrum" he had a splendid partner its Ashurst. They were opposed by two capable men in Dunbavin and Molyneux and it is questionable whether four better half-backs have been seen in a club match before. The score was opened in rather a startling manner. Dunbavin was making one of his usual drop- kicks when Bate charged the ball down and coming into possession el it he dodged over the line. Dunbavin, as though to compensate for the loss he had been the means of inflicting on his side,redoubled his effArts and success rewarded him at length in seeing the ball flying over the coveted crosv -bar. This only served as a stimulus to the Warrington men to increase the lead and just before the whistle sounded for half-time Duck- worth justified his inclusion in the team by scoring a try. Fish further increased the lead by kicking a goal.

With a lead of eight points against two, the Warring- ton spectators seemed pr,tty confidnit of victory. Bat their anticipations were rudely upset. Leigh made full use of the wind and sun and at the close of the match they were two points ahead. Warrington were most unfortunate in first losing the serviceq of Fish and then Harris through injuries, and it is possible that if they had been able to continue to the end of the match the result might have been different. The Leigh team cannot be described- as a brilliant combination. Danbavin is undoubtedly a first-class man and the same remark will perhaps apply to Molyneux but the others are a moderate lot. The forwards are exceptionally strong, but until Warrington were obliged to bring a man out of the pack, they gained little advantage over them. Bate and Fish were the smartest men on the Warrington side but it may be generously , conceded that all the Warrington men did , themselves justice with the exception of Siddall and I Hallam. Siddall failed to open out the game and he was " held" time after time with the ball in his possession. Hallam did not play one of his great games, and was dreadfully slow in returning the ball on many oc- casions. With these exceptions little fault could be found with the Warrington fifteen, and although beaten they were by no means disgraced.

Fletcher Russell's were at home to Runcorn Rees on Saturday last, and bad absentees in Scott Brown and Forster, but playing with dogged determination which is a feature of the Engineers play,secured a grand victory by 5 tries to one. A new wing was tried in Plinston who last year played with a local junior team, and be scored 3 tries out of sin clever fashion, and is included in the Engineers team against St. Mary's to-day. We think this lad will make an exc.llent wing player, and playing with the Engineers will have the chance of learning at the tricks of the trade. Fletcher's committee are cer- tainly to be commended for their enterprise, as they never seem to be stuck for a player, whenever they re- quire a vacancy filling, they always have a man fit for the position to place in the field. Such energy wouldibe very acceptable elsewhere. Fletcher's team for to-day against St. Mary's at Lovely•lane is as follows .—Full back, T Knight ; three-quarters, rlisston, Lawless, Nel- son, Radford ; half backs, Foden, Sutton ; forwards, T Antrobus, J Berry, Warburton, Jolley, Spencer, Davies, Holbrook and Brown.

News is to hand by mal of the brilliant play of Mr. J. S. Francomb, a member of the Eagrish team now tour- ing in Australia and New Zealand. He is described as one of the most brilliant forwards seen in international contests, and his play both in the pack and loose has won for him high encomiums. Our readers may remem- ber that Mr. Francomb has won laurels with the Lanca- shire team. He is the son of Alderman Francomb, of Victoria House, Wilderspool, and we understand he intends to settle in Australia for a lengthened period.

CRICKET.

The season is now quickly drawing to a clos9. War- rington played Sefton on their own ground on Saturday, this being tne concluding match on the Sefton card. The visitors were short of two good men in Callon and Connor. Sefton won the toss. and batting first on a slow wicket, compiled 106, Jones and H. H. Boswell knocking up 23 and 20 respectively. The Warrington men were dismissed for the small total of 59.805we1l and Wilkinson working great havoc with the ball.

INQUEST AND VERDICT.

The inquest on the body ot J,I3fV) Cordingley Wilson, an engineer. of Radnor-street, FluErne, Manchester, who met his death as a resolt of injures ansitaitiel in the railway collision at Victoria Station on Monday. was resumed by the Manchester city deputy coroner, Mr. Aitken, on Thursday inoraing.—E iwin Bsoth, the driver of the train, said he went into the station at a walking pace, four or five miles an hour. It wad a perfectly safe speed to go at. He went on to explain that if he had had another tea yards to go he would have prevented the acciden% He applied toe brake as soon as he was aware of the proximity of the Blackpool train. The signal was right tor hi,ii to go ahead.—The jury form.' chat the accident was caused by a mistake on the part of one of the signalmen on duty, but by whom they were unable to say. ANOTHER POLICE SCINDSL IN MANCHEBTER. RESIGNATION OF A SUPERINTENDENT.

The Manche icer Watch Committee were engaged yes- terday morning in the investigatior, of a serious com- plaint relating to the conduct of a number of members of the B Division, inciultig ithe superintendent of tnat part of the ince. The all ,gation was that the men concerned had been in the habit of receiving Christmas presents from firms who are not unconnected with the brewing industry. It was reported to the committee that the superintendent had sent in his resignation. A question arose regarding his pension,and on this account and also for reasons which were based upon the gravity of the case and the number of offic-?rs involved the committee deferred their decision for a week. The superintendent of the division is Mr R Smith, who was appointed only a comparatively short time ago upon the resiftmation of Superintendent Go by. LATEST LOCAL WEATHER FORECAST.

PANIC IN A SYNAGOGUE.

32 WOMEN AND CHILD LIEN CRUSHED TO -DEATH.

BERLIN, THURSDAY.

A telegram from Kalisch, in Russian Poland, states that a terrible panic occurred in a synagegue at Lents- chitry owing to the upartting of a lamp. Thirty-two persons, all women and children,were crushed to death, and many were injured. THE HOUR CYCLE RECORD BROKEN. PHILADELPHIA, THURSDAY. Eckes has broken the world's hour bicycle record by 747 yards. FALL OF A TRAIN INTO A. RIVER, FIVE RAILWAY OFFICIALS KILLED. MUNICH, THURSDAY. A fatal railway accident has occurred between Muchldorf and Rohrbach. Owing to the undermining of one of the piers of a bridge over the Isen through the floods a train which was crossing fell into the river. Five railway officials were killed. There were no pas- sengers on the train.

DEATH OF MR. J. E. WORSLEY. • It is with regret that we recorl the death of Mr. J. E. Worsley, solicitor, and coroner for the Honor and Fee _of Halton which took place on Wednesday night at his residence, the Hermitage. Winwick. His death was due to choleraic diarrhcea. He had been indi4oosed for about a fortnight, and the comparative suddenness and unexpectedness of • his death came as a paitlful surprise to his many friends. The deceased gentleman was brought prominently before the public of Runcorn and dis- trict in his capacity of coroner, and by his kindly disposition and sympa'hetic treatment of the cases which came before him he had endeared himself to many and gained the respect of all with whom he came in contact in carrying out his sometimes painful duties. Up to within a recent period Mt. Worsley also fulfilled the duties of Clerk to the Newton Commissioners, and when that authority became the Newton-in-Makerfield Urban District Council, Mr. Worsley was appointed law clerk.. A few weeks ago he appeared at the Warrington County Court on behalf of the Newton Council in an important tollage case, and gave notice of appeal against:the)judge's decision. For many years he was a partner in the firm of Messrs. Ridgway • and Worsley, solicitors, Cairo-street, but two years ago the partnership was dissolved, and Mr. WorsleY then practised alone,having offices in Horsemarket- street. Ile did not take a prominent part in public affairs, and to a great extent he lived retired life. He was, however, a prominent figure among local antiquarians, and succeeded the late Mr. William Beamont as an authority upon antiquarian subjects. He was one of the non- members of the Town Council appointed on the Museum, Library, Arts, and Technical Instruction Committee. He was a regular worshipper at St. Oswald's Church, Winwick, in connection with which he has discharged the duties of churchwar- den for many years. He took a great interest in all that concerned this historic church. He was 59 years of age, and leaves a widow and six cbildren--' two sons and four daughters. The funeral service takes place this (FridaY) Merning at Winwick Church at ten o'clock. REFERENCE AT NEWTON SESSIONS. Before the commencement of the ordinary busi- ness at the Newton Petty Sessions on TeursdaY • morning, the Chairman (Captain Withington) said that he wished to express on behalf of himself and his brother magistrates their feelings of loss sue- tained by the death of a solicitor on the previous day, who had practised most courteously in that court ;he referred to Mr. Worsley. He thought 50 intimation of their sorrow should be sent to de- ceased's relatives. Mr. J. W. Knowles, on behalf of the solicitors practising at the court, said that they deeply synr pathised with the family in the loss they had sus- tained. He was sure that they and the public of the district would feel very much grieved at the loss of Mr. Worsley such a comparatively young age.

WHY "SARAH GRA.ND'S " PEN-NAME WAS CHOSEN. ---- " Cassell's. Saturday Journal" asks, " Why do so many authors affect pseudonyms, as if they were fear- ful of identifying themselves with tneir own work? And whence come these familiar noms de querre ? With a view of throwing some light on this interesting subject, we recently put ourselves into communication with a few well-known writers who are known to fame by names other than their own. Ot th 3 ladies two questions were asked—(l)—how they were led to adopt their re- spective pseudonyms; and (2) whe:her there is any ad- vantage in a lady using a mascohne appellation at the commencement of her career. Scribes of the opposite sex were Elimply interrogated as to the origin of their pea-names. "A11&11 GRAND," whose real name is Mrs. M'Fall, observes : "'Sarah Grand' is not a noru de querre, but a change of name. My husband was 11,i:. in sympathy with my views on social subjects and naturally objecAsd to have his name attached to them, and ac there was no other way to save him tue annoyance I took the name of Sarah Grand,' and since then I hava usr:d no other. My choice of the name i:self when it occurred to me was decided by its plainness. " I do not think that a masculine pseudonym is any help to women nowadays. When George Elliot and George Sand wrath it was necessary because of the scant courtesy with which women were treated. Now it is sometimes assumed that a woman may have intellect until sbe proves she has none. Besides, there is no escaping publicity now." It will be remembered by many Warrington people that the late Dr. M'Fall lived in Folly-lane. Warrington, for some years.
